* **Pierre Cardaliaguet, François Delarue, Jean-Michel Lasry, and
  Pierre-Louis Lions, *The Master Equation and the Convergence Problem
  in Mean Field Games*** (2019, [Princeton University Press](https://doi.org/10.23943/princeton/9780691190716.001.0001)). Listed in full in Section 8.
* **Augusto C. Ponce, *Elliptic PDEs, Measures and Capacities*** (2016,
  [European Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/140)). Listed in full in Section 26.
* **Pascal Auscher and Moritz Egert, *Boundary Value Problems and Hardy
  Spaces for Elliptic Systems with Block Structure*** (2023,
  [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-29973-5)). Listed in full in Section 25.
* **Mourad Choulli, *Applications of Elliptic Carleman Inequalities to
  Cauchy and Inverse Problems*** (2016, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-33642-8)). Carleman estimates
  as a tool for unique continuation and inverse problems.

---

<a id="ckma-34"></a>

## 34. Hyperbolic Equations, Conservation Laws, Dispersive PDE, Fluids, and Kinetic Theory

Nonlinear evolution PDE are organised into hyperbolic systems and
conservation laws (34A), dispersive equations and nonlinear waves (34B),
mathematical fluid dynamics (34C), and kinetic equations (34D). Their
connections include shock formation, weak solutions, dispersion, transport,
and hydrodynamic limits, while their distinct solution concepts and
estimates remain explicit. Sections 26–30 provide analytic tools, Section 32
general linear evolution theory, Section 9 stochastic counterparts, and
Section 51 integrable models and inverse-scattering methods.

<a id="ckma-34a"></a>

### 34A. Hyperbolic Systems and Conservation Laws

Characteristics, energy estimates, shock formation, entropy solutions, and
weak compactness describe hyperbolic propagation and discontinuous
evolution.

#### Foundational works

* **Constantine M. Dafermos, *Hyperbolic Conservation Laws in
  Continuum Physics***. The encyclopedic research reference: entropy
  solutions, shocks, BV methods, and continuum-mechanical structure.

* **Alberto Bressan, *Hyperbolic Systems of Conservation Laws: The
  One-Dimensional Cauchy Problem***. Front tracking, semigroup
  solutions, and stability.

* **Helge Holden and Nils Henrik Risebro, *Front Tracking for
  Hyperbolic Conservation Laws*** (2nd ed., 2015, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-662-47507-2)). Front
  tracking as an existence method, updated.

* **Denis Serre, *Systems of Conservation Laws I–II***. A broad
  analytic and structural treatment.

* **Peter D. Lax, *Hyperbolic Systems of Conservation Laws and the
  Mathematical Theory of Shock Waves***. The short foundational
  monograph.

* **Philippe G. LeFloch, *Hyperbolic Systems of Conservation Laws: The
  Theory of Classical and Nonclassical Shock Waves*** (2002,
  [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-0348-8150-0)). Undercompressive
  shocks, kinetic relations, and well-posedness in the nonclassical regime.

* **Andrew Majda, *Compressible Fluid Flow and Systems of Conservation
  Laws in Several Space Variables***. Hyperbolic systems from the
  compressible-flow viewpoint.

* **Sylvie Benzoni-Gavage and Denis Serre, *Multidimensional Hyperbolic
  Partial Differential Equations: First-Order Systems and Applications***
  (2007, [Oxford University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1093/acprof:oso/9780199211234.001.0001)).
  Well-posedness of hyperbolic initial–boundary value problems, Kreiss
  symmetrizers, and the stability of shocks.

* **Lars Hörmander, *Lectures on Nonlinear Hyperbolic Differential
  Equations***. High-level energy and microlocal methods for nonlinear
  waves.

* **Serge Alinhac, *Hyperbolic Partial Differential Equations***. A short
  graduate introduction to hyperbolic equations: characteristics, energy
  estimates, and well-posedness. His *Geometric Analysis of Hyperbolic
  Differential Equations* is listed among the more recent works.

* **Joel Smoller, *Shock Waves and Reaction–Diffusion Equations***.
  Conservation laws, shock waves, reaction–diffusion equations, and the
  Conley index. The classical graduate text.

#### More recent works

* **Serge Alinhac, *Geometric Analysis of Hyperbolic Differential
  Equations: An Introduction*** (2010, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9781139107198), London Mathematical
  Society Lecture Note Series). Vector-field methods, energy inequalities,
  and the null condition for small-data global existence of quasilinear
  wave equations.

* **Demetrios Christodoulou, *The Shock Development Problem*** (2019,
  [European Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/192)). The free-boundary problem for the
  development of shocks in compressible Euler flow, continuing his
  *Formation of Shocks in 3-Dimensional Fluids* (2007, European
  Mathematical Society).

* **Jared Speck, *Shock Formation in Small-Data Solutions to 3D
  Quasilinear Wave Equations*** (2016, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/surv/214),
  Mathematical Surveys and Monographs). Shock formation for quasilinear
  waves, in the Christodoulou framework.

<a id="ckma-34b"></a>

### 34B. Dispersive Equations and Nonlinear Waves

Dispersion, spacetime estimates, critical regularity, concentration, and
stability organise nonlinear wave and Schrödinger-type equations, including
geometric wave systems.

#### Foundational works

* **Terence Tao, *Nonlinear Dispersive Equations: Local and Global
  Analysis***. The modern toolkit: Strichartz estimates, conservation
  laws, concentration compactness, and criticality.

* **Thierry Cazenave, *Semilinear Schrödinger Equations***. The
  standard monograph for nonlinear Schrödinger equations.

* **Jean Bourgain, *Global Solutions of Nonlinear Schrödinger
  Equations***. Global well-posedness for nonlinear Schrödinger equations,
  in the AMS Colloquium Publications monograph. Distinct from his *Green's
  Function Estimates for Lattice Schrödinger Operators*.

* **Felipe Linares and Gustavo Ponce, *Introduction to Nonlinear
  Dispersive Equations***. A graduate introduction to dispersive estimates
  and well-posedness methods for nonlinear evolution equations, including
  KdV and nonlinear Schrödinger equations.

* **Christopher D. Sogge, *Lectures on Nonlinear Wave
  Equations***. Energy and dispersive estimates for nonlinear waves.

* **Jalal Shatah and Michael Struwe, *Geometric Wave
  Equations***. Wave maps and geometric hyperbolic PDE.

* **Sergiu Klainerman and Francesco Nicolò, *The Evolution Problem in
  General Relativity***, and **Demetrios Christodoulou and Sergiu
  Klainerman, *The Global Nonlinear Stability of the Minkowski
  Space***. Global stability of Minkowski space and the Einstein vacuum
  equations, using geometric formulations and estimates for nonlinear
  hyperbolic systems.

* **Yvonne Choquet-Bruhat, *General Relativity and the Einstein Equations***
  (2009, [Oxford University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1093/acprof:oso/9780199230723.001.0001)).
  The Einstein equations, constraint equations, and their Cauchy problem,
  with appendices developing the required analytic and geometric tools.

* **Hans Ringström, *The Cauchy Problem in General Relativity*** (2009,
  [European Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/053)). Local
  existence, maximal globally hyperbolic developments, and the hyperbolic
  PDE theory they require.

* **Demetrios Christodoulou, *The Formation of Black Holes in General
  Relativity*** (2009, [European Mathematical
  Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/068)). Trapped-surface formation for the
  vacuum Einstein equations from regular data. A landmark of hyperbolic PDE.

* **Reinhard Racke, *Lectures on Nonlinear Evolution Equations: Initial
  Value Problems***. Global existence for small data in nonlinear wave,
  heat, and elasticity equations.

#### More recent works

* **Hajer Bahouri, Jean-Yves Chemin, and Raphaël Danchin, *Fourier
  Analysis and Nonlinear Partial Differential
  Equations*** (2011, Springer). Littlewood–Paley and paradifferential
  methods applied systematically to nonlinear PDE; now the standard
  technical reference. Also listed in Section 30.

* **M. Burak Erdoğan and Nikolaos Tzirakis, *Dispersive Partial Differential
  Equations: Wellposedness and Applications*** (2016, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9781316563267)). Fourier restriction
  norms, smoothing, and low-regularity well-posedness. A graduate account
  concentrating on techniques for nonlinear dispersive equations.

* **Herbert Koch, Daniel Tataru, and Monica Vişan, *Dispersive
  Equations and Nonlinear Waves*** (2014, Birkhäuser). Three sets of
  lecture notes: Koch on dispersive and Strichartz estimates, U^p and V^p
  spaces, and well-posedness; Tataru on wave maps and Schrödinger maps; and
  Vişan on concentration compactness
  and profile decompositions for nonlinear Schrödinger equations.

* **Carlos E. Kenig, *Lectures on the Energy Critical Nonlinear Wave
  Equation*** (2015, [American Mathematical
  Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/cbms/122), CBMS Regional Conference
  Series). Concentration compactness and rigidity, and the soliton
  resolution programme for the energy-critical wave equation.

* **Kenji Nakanishi and Wilhelm Schlag, *Invariant Manifolds and
  Dispersive Hamiltonian Evolution Equations*** (2011, [European
  Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/095)). Invariant manifolds for nonlinear Klein–Gordon
  and Schrödinger equations.

* **Joachim Krieger and Wilhelm Schlag, *Concentration Compactness for
  Critical Wave Maps*** (2012, [European Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/106)).
  Global regularity and scattering for energy-critical wave maps into the
  hyperbolic plane, proved by concentration compactness.

* **Christian Klein and Jean-Claude Saut, *Nonlinear Dispersive Equations:
  Inverse Scattering and PDE Methods*** (2022; copyright 2021,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-91427-1)). Dispersive equations
  treated through both inverse scattering and PDE estimates, with emphasis on
  Benjamin–Ono, Davey–Stewartson, and Kadomtsev–Petviashvili models. Useful
  for comparing integrable and nonintegrable behaviour.

* **Todd Kapitula and Keith Promislow, *Spectral and Dynamical Stability of
  Nonlinear Waves*** (2013,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4614-6995-7)). Evans functions,
  Hamiltonian spectral theory, and orbital stability. A systematic bridge from
  linearized spectra to the dynamics of nonlinear waves.

* **Gadi Fibich, *The Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation: Singular Solutions
  and Optical Collapse*** (2015,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-12748-4)). Blow-up and
  collapse for the nonlinear Schrödinger equation, combining rigorous and
  asymptotic analysis.

<a id="ckma-34c"></a>

### 34C. Mathematical Fluid Dynamics

Euler, Navier–Stokes, compressible flow, water waves, and turbulence are
studied through existence, regularity, vorticity, stability, and singular
limits.

#### Foundational works

* **Roger Temam, *Navier–Stokes Equations***. The classical
  functional-analytic treatment of incompressible flow.

* **Giovanni P. Galdi, *An Introduction to the Mathematical Theory of
  the Navier–Stokes Equations***. A detailed reference for stationary
  incompressible Navier–Stokes equations, including existence, uniqueness,
  regularity, and problems in exterior domains.

* **Andrew Majda and Andrea Bertozzi, *Vorticity and Incompressible
  Flow***. Euler and Navier–Stokes through vorticity, transport, and
  harmonic analysis.

* **Jean-Yves Chemin, *Perfect Incompressible
  Fluids***. Paradifferential and geometric methods for the Euler
  equations.

* **Carlo Marchioro and Mario Pulvirenti, *Mathematical Theory of
  Incompressible Nonviscous Fluids***. The Euler equations through the
  vortex method and the point-vortex system.

* **Pierre-Louis Lions, *Mathematical Topics in Fluid Mechanics
  I–II***. Incompressible and compressible flow from the weak-solution
  viewpoint.

* **Peter Constantin and Ciprian Foias, *Navier–Stokes
  Equations***. Dynamical-systems and functional-analytic aspects.

* **Ciprian Foias, Oscar Manley, Ricardo Rosa, and Roger Temam,
  *Navier–Stokes Equations and Turbulence*** (2001, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511546754)). Statistical solutions,
  and the connection between the mathematical theory of the Navier–Stokes
  equations and conventional turbulence theory.

* **Marco Cannone, *Ondelettes, paraproduits et
  Navier–Stokes***. Critical spaces and harmonic-analytic
  well-posedness.

* **Jean-Yves Chemin, Benoit Desjardins, Isabelle Gallagher, and
  Emmanuel Grenier, *Mathematical Geophysics: An Introduction to
  Rotating Fluids and the Navier–Stokes Equations*** (2006, [Oxford
  University Press](https://doi.org/10.1093/oso/9780198571339.001.0001)).
  Incompressible Navier–Stokes equations, rapid-rotation limits, and
  boundary-layer effects in geophysical fluid models.

* **Eduard Feireisl and Antonín Novotný, *Singular Limits in Thermodynamics
  of Viscous Fluids*** (2nd ed., 2017,
  [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-63781-5)). Weak solutions
  of compressible Navier–Stokes–Fourier systems and their low-Mach-number
  and other singular limits.

* **Charles R. Doering and J. D. Gibbon, *Applied Analysis of the
  Navier–Stokes Equations***. Energy estimates, attractors, and regularity
  questions for the Navier–Stokes equations.

#### More recent works

* **Jacob Bedrossian and Vlad Vicol, *The Mathematical Analysis of the
  Incompressible Euler and Navier–Stokes Equations: An Introduction***
  (2022, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/225), Graduate Studies in Mathematics).
  A one-semester graduate introduction to well-posedness, vorticity, and
  blow-up criteria.

* **Franck Boyer and Pierre Fabrie, *Mathematical Tools for the Study of the
  Incompressible Navier–Stokes Equations and Related Models*** (2013,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4614-5975-0)). Sobolev spaces, weak
  formulations, compactness, and evolution estimates for viscous flow. Useful
  for learning the analytic infrastructure before more specialised fluid
  monographs.

* **James C. Robinson, José L. Rodrigo, and Witold Sadowski, *The
  Three-Dimensional Navier–Stokes Equations: Classical Theory*** (2016,
  [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9781139095143)). Mild solutions, weak solutions, and the
  classical regularity theory in one volume.

* **Tai-Peng Tsai, *Lectures on Navier–Stokes Equations*** (2018, [American
  Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/192), Graduate Studies
  in Mathematics). Weak and mild solutions, partial regularity, self-similar
  solutions, and ε-regularity.

* **Pierre Gilles Lemarié-Rieusset, *The Navier–Stokes Problem in the
  21st Century*** (2016, CRC Press). A comprehensive modern account
  organized around the regularity problem.

* **David Lannes, *The Water Waves Problem: Mathematical Analysis and
  Asymptotics*** (2013, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/surv/188), Mathematical
  Surveys and Monographs). The well-posedness and asymptotic theory of
  the water-wave equations.
  Also listed in Section 51.

* **Sergei B. Kuksin and Armen Shirikyan, *Mathematics of Two-Dimensional
  Turbulence*** (2012, [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9781139137119)). Invariant measures
  for the stochastic two-dimensional Navier–Stokes equations.
  Also listed in Section 9.

* **Dominic Breit, Eduard Feireisl, and Martina Hofmanová, *Stochastically
  Forced Compressible Fluid Flows*** (2018, [De
  Gruyter](https://doi.org/10.1515/9783110492552)). Martingale solutions,
  compactness, and stochastic forcing in compressible fluid equations. A
  research monograph joining nonlinear PDE with stochastic evolution.

* **Eduard Feireisl, Trygve G. Karper, and Milan Pokorný, *Mathematical Theory of
  Compressible Viscous Fluids: Analysis and Numerics*** (2016,
  [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-44835-0)). Weak solutions of the compressible
  Navier–Stokes system and the analysis of their approximation. Distinct from Feireisl and
  Novotný, *Singular Limits in Thermodynamics of Viscous Fluids*.

<a id="ckma-34d"></a>

### 34D. Kinetic Equations

Particle and velocity-distribution descriptions, collision operators,
entropy, kinetic formulations, and hydrodynamic limits connect microscopic
dynamics with continuum equations.

#### Foundational works

* **Carlo Cercignani, Reinhard Illner, and Mario Pulvirenti, *The
  Mathematical Theory of Dilute Gases***. The standard reference for
  Boltzmann-equation analysis.

* **Cédric Villani, *A Review of Mathematical Topics in Collisional
  Kinetic Theory*** (2002, chapter in *Handbook of Mathematical Fluid
  Dynamics*, volume 1,
  [North-Holland](https://doi.org/10.1016/S1874-5792(02)80004-0), pp. 71–305).
  A survey of collisional kinetic equations, including Boltzmann and Landau
  equations, entropy methods, regularity, and hydrodynamic limits.

* **Robert T. Glassey, *The Cauchy Problem in Kinetic Theory***. A
  careful treatment of Vlasov and Boltzmann well-posedness.

* **Laure Saint-Raymond, *Hydrodynamic Limits of the Boltzmann
  Equation*** (2009, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-540-92847-8), Lecture Notes in Mathematics). The
  passage from Boltzmann to fluid equations.

* **Benoît Perthame, *Kinetic Formulation of Conservation Laws*** (2002,
  [Oxford University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1093/oso/9780198509134.001.0001)). Kinetic
  formulations of scalar conservation laws and the compactness they give.
  The analytic reference for the method.

* **Luc Tartar, *From Hyperbolic Systems to Kinetic Theory***. Compensated compactness and
  the passage from hyperbolic systems of conservation laws to kinetic equations. Distinct
  from his *The General Theory of Homogenization*.

* **Carlo Cercignani and Gilberto Medeiros Kremer, *The Relativistic Boltzmann Equation:
  Theory and Applications***. The relativistic Boltzmann equation and its fluid limits.
  Distinct from Cercignani, Illner, and Pulvirenti, *The Mathematical Theory of Dilute
  Gases*.

#### More recent works

* **Isabelle Gallagher, Laure Saint-Raymond, and Benjamin Texier, *From
  Newton to Boltzmann: Hard Spheres and Short-range Potentials***
  (2014, [European Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/129), Zurich Lectures in Advanced
  Mathematics). Lanford's theorem and the low-density limit, in a
  self-contained monograph.

* **Alexander V. Bobylev, *Kinetic Equations, Volume 1: Boltzmann Equation, Maxwell Models,
  and Hydrodynamics beyond Navier–Stokes*** (2020, [De
  Gruyter](https://doi.org/10.1515/9783110550986)). The Boltzmann equation for Maxwell
  models, and hydrodynamic descriptions beyond the Navier–Stokes level.


---

<a id="ckma-35"></a>

## 35. Nonlinear Functional Analysis, Monotone Operators, and Fixed Points

Degree theory, fixed points, monotone operators, nonlinear spectral methods,
and bifurcation provide abstract existence and continuation principles for
nonlinear problems. Variational and order methods are included where they
contribute to this functional-analytic framework. Section 12 supplies convex
and nonsmooth calculus and optimization, Section 24 develops abstract
evolution, and Section 36 treats minimization and critical points of
functionals on function spaces. Applications to particular differential
equations remain with their PDE or dynamical sections.

### Foundational works

* **Klaus Deimling, *Nonlinear Functional Analysis***. A standard
  graduate text on degree theory, fixed points, monotone operators,
  and nonlinear equations.
* **Eberhard Zeidler, *Nonlinear Functional Analysis and Its
  Applications I–IV*** (five volumes, Part II in two) and ***Applied
  Functional Analysis*** (2 volumes). Encyclopedic coverage of fixed points, monotonicity,
  variational methods, and applications.
* **Louis Nirenberg, *Topics in Nonlinear Functional
  Analysis***. Short, influential notes on degree, implicit-function
  methods, and bifurcation.
* **Steven G. Krantz and Harold R. Parks, *The Implicit Function Theorem:
  History, Theory, and Applications***. The implicit-function theorem from
  the classical statement through the nonsmooth and Nash–Moser forms.
* **Thierry Cazenave and Alain Haraux, *An Introduction to Semilinear
  Evolution Equations***. Listed in full in Section 24.
* **Kung-Ching Chang, *Methods in Nonlinear Analysis*** (2005,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/3-540-29232-2), Springer Monographs in
  Mathematics). Linearisation, fixed points, degree, minimax, and Morse
  theory, with applications, in one volume.
* **Pavel Drábek and Jaroslav Milota, *Methods of Nonlinear Analysis:
  Applications to Differential Equations*** (2nd ed., 2013,
  [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-0348-0387-8)). Degree,
  bifurcation, variational methods, and nonlinear differential equations in
  one graduate reference. A broad practical companion to the abstract
  nonlinear-analysis classics.
* **R. E. Showalter, *Monotone Operators in Banach Space and Nonlinear
  Partial Differential Equations***. A systematic treatment of
  monotonicity and nonlinear evolution.
* **Haim Brezis, *Opérateurs maximaux monotones et semi-groupes de
  contractions dans les espaces de Hilbert***. The foundational
  monograph on maximal monotone operators and nonlinear semigroups.
* **Viorel Barbu, *Nonlinear Semigroups and Differential Equations in
  Banach Spaces***. Monotone methods in nonlinear evolution equations. Its
  successor, *Nonlinear Differential Equations of Monotone Types in Banach
  Spaces* (2010), is listed in Section 24.
* **Stephen Simons, *From Hahn–Banach to Monotonicity*** (2nd ed., 2008,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4020-6919-2)). Listed in full in Section 12.
* **Regina S. Burachik and Alfredo N. Iusem, *Set-Valued Mappings and
  Enlargements of Monotone Operators*** (2008, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-387-69757-4)). Listed in full in Section 12.
* **M. A. Krasnosel'skii, *Topological Methods in the Theory of
  Nonlinear Integral Equations***, and **Krasnosel'skii and Zabreiko,
  *Geometrical Methods of Nonlinear Analysis***. The classical
  topological fixed-point tradition.
* **Andrzej Granas and James Dugundji, *Fixed Point Theory***. A
  comprehensive topological treatment.
* **Kazimierz Goebel and W. A. Kirk, *Topics in Metric Fixed Point
  Theory***. Nonexpansive mappings and the geometry of Banach spaces.
* **N. G. Lloyd, *Degree Theory***, and **Jean Mawhin, *Topological
  Degree Methods in Nonlinear Boundary Value Problems*** (CBMS 40). The
  standard short references for topological degree and coincidence
  degree.
* **Roger D. Nussbaum, *The Fixed Point Index and Some
  Applications***. A research-level reference on index methods.
* **Melvyn S. Berger, *Nonlinearity and Functional Analysis***. A
  broad older synthesis, still useful for its range of applications.
* **Shui-Nee Chow and Jack K. Hale, *Methods of Bifurcation
  Theory***. The analytic theory of bifurcation in infinite
  dimensions.
* **Hansjörg Kielhöfer, *Bifurcation Theory: An Introduction with
  Applications to Partial Differential Equations*** (2nd ed., 2012,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4614-0502-3)). Local and global
  bifurcation, the Crandall–Rabinowitz theorem, Hopf bifurcation, and degree
  methods, applied to elliptic and parabolic problems.
* **Boris Buffoni and John Toland, *Analytic Theory of Global Bifurcation:
  An Introduction*** (2003, [Princeton University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1515/9781400884339)). Global bifurcation in
  real-analytic settings, with water waves as the motivating application.
* **Tomáš Roubíček, *Nonlinear Partial Differential Equations with
  Applications*** (2nd ed., 2013,
  [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-0348-0513-1)). Monotone
  operators, weak solutions, compactness, and nonlinear evolution with
  applications. A graduate-to-research synthesis of functional-analytic PDE
  methods.
* **Jürgen Appell, Espedito De Pascale, and Alfonso Vignoli, *Nonlinear Spectral Theory***.
  Spectra of nonlinear operators, nonlinear eigenvalues, and the comparison with the linear
  spectral theory.
* **Jorge Ize and Alfonso Vignoli, *Equivariant Degree Theory***. Topological degree for
  equivariant maps, and bifurcation in the presence of symmetry.

### More recent works

* **Boris M. Makarov and Anatolii N. Podkorytov, *Smooth Functions and
  Maps*** (2021; English translation,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-79438-5)). Smooth calculus in
  finite dimensions: inverse and implicit functions, critical points, and
  singularities. The finite-dimensional smooth theory before the
  infinite-dimensional implicit-function theorems.
* **Antonio Ambrosetti and David Arcoya, *An Introduction to Nonlinear
  Functional Analysis and Elliptic Problems*** (2011, [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-8176-8114-2)).
  Degree, bifurcation, and variational methods aimed at elliptic
  equations.
* **George Dinca and Jean Mawhin, *Brouwer Degree: The Core of Nonlinear
  Analysis*** (2021, [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-63230-4)). A modern reconstruction of Brouwer
  degree as the root of the topological theory.
* **Heinz H. Bauschke and Patrick L. Combettes, *Convex Analysis and
  Monotone Operator Theory in Hilbert Spaces*** (2011, Springer). The modern synthesis
  of monotone operators, resolvents, and proximal methods.
  Also listed in Section 12.
* **Nikolaos S. Papageorgiou, Vicenţiu D. Rădulescu, and Dušan D. Repovš,
  *Nonlinear Analysis — Theory and Methods*** (2019,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-03430-6), Springer Monographs
  in Mathematics). A large synthesis of nonlinear operators, degree theory,
  critical point theory, and Morse theory, with applications to problems
  driven by the p-Laplacian.
* **Zhitao Zhang, *Variational, Topological, and Partial Order Methods with
  Their Applications*** (2013,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-30709-6)). Variational
  arguments, degree theory, and cone methods for nonlinear equations. Useful
  for comparing several existence techniques within the same applications.
* **Martin Väth, *Topological Analysis: From the Basics to the Triple Degree
  for Nonlinear Fredholm Inclusions*** (2012, [De
  Gruyter](https://doi.org/10.1515/9783110277333)). Topological degree,
  set-valued maps, and nonlinear Fredholm problems. A specialised extension of
  the standard single-valued degree theory.
* **Jean-Paul Penot, *Calculus Without Derivatives*** (2013, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4614-4538-8)).
  Listed in full in Section 12.
* **Juan Peypouquet, *Convex Optimization in Normed Spaces*** (2015,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-13710-0)). Listed in full in Section 12.

---

<a id="ckma-36"></a>

## 36. Calculus of Variations, Γ-Convergence, and Homogenization

Direct methods, lower semicontinuity, relaxation, critical-point theory,
Γ-convergence, and homogenization describe variational problems and their
limiting behaviour. Shape optimization, optimal control, Hamiltonian
variational methods, vortices, composites, and rate-independent evolution
are included through their functional and compactness structures. Section 12
develops convex optimization and generalized calculus, Section 35 abstract
nonlinear methods, and Section 37 BV functions, currents, and geometric
compactness. Optimal transport and metric gradient flows have a
complementary home in Section 39C.

### Foundational works

* **Bernard Dacorogna, *Direct Methods in the Calculus of
  Variations***. The standard treatment of weak lower semicontinuity,
  convexity, polyconvexity, and quasiconvexity.
* **Charles B. Morrey, Jr., *Multiple Integrals in the Calculus of
  Variations***. The foundational monograph for quasiconvexity and
  regularity of multiple integrals.
* **Mariano Giaquinta and Stefan Hildebrandt, *Calculus of Variations
  I–II***. A broad classical treatment of direct methods, regularity,
  and geometric problems.
* **Enrico Giusti, *Direct Methods in the Calculus of
  Variations***. Concise and classical; an excellent entry point.
  Also listed in Section 33.
* **Irene Fonseca and Giovanni Leoni, *Modern Methods in the Calculus
  of Variations: L^p Spaces***. A rigorous modern route through lower
  semicontinuity, relaxation, and Young measures.
* **Ivar Ekeland and Roger Temam, *Convex Analysis and Variational
  Problems***. Convex duality and variational principles with PDE
  applications.
  Also listed in Section 12.
* **Hedy Attouch, Giuseppe Buttazzo, and Gérard Michaille, *Variational
  Analysis in Sobolev and BV Spaces*** (2nd ed., 2014, [SIAM](https://doi.org/10.1137/1.9781611973488)). Listed in full in Section 12.
* **Michael Struwe, *Variational Methods***. Critical point theory,
  concentration compactness, Palais–Smale conditions, and geometric
  PDE.
* **Paul H. Rabinowitz, *Minimax Methods in Critical Point Theory with
  Applications to Differential Equations***. A concise treatment of minimax
  methods and critical-point theory, with applications to nonlinear
  differential equations.
* **Michel Willem, *Minimax Theorems***. A compact systematic
  reference for modern critical-point arguments.
* **Antonio Ambrosetti and Andrea Malchiodi, *Nonlinear Analysis and
  Semilinear Elliptic Problems***. Variational and perturbative
  methods for elliptic equations.
* **Jean Mawhin and Michel Willem, *Critical Point Theory and
  Hamiltonian Systems***. The standard reference on the Hamiltonian
  side.
* **Andrea Braides, *Γ-Convergence for Beginners***. An introductory course in
  Γ-convergence and its use in variational limits, with examples from
  homogenization and related problems.
* **Gianni Dal Maso, *An Introduction to Γ-Convergence***. A systematic
  reference for Γ-convergence, integral representation, relaxation, and
  applications to variational problems.
* **Andrea Braides and Anneliese Defranceschi, *Homogenization of
  Multiple Integrals***. Γ-convergence applied to homogenization.
* **Alain Bensoussan, Jacques-Louis Lions, and George Papanicolaou,
  *Asymptotic Analysis for Periodic Structures***. The founding
  treatise of mathematical homogenization.
* **V. V. Jikov, S. M. Kozlov, and O. A. Oleinik, *Homogenization of
  Differential Operators and Integral Functionals***. The
  comprehensive reference, including random and degenerate cases.
* **Doina Cioranescu and Patrizia Donato, *An Introduction to
  Homogenization***. A readable modern textbook.
* **Luc Tartar, *The General Theory of Homogenization: A Personalized
  Introduction***. Compensated compactness and H-measures from one of
  the originators.
* **Grigorios A. Pavliotis and Andrew M. Stuart, *Multiscale Methods:
  Averaging and Homogenization*** (2008,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-387-73829-1), Texts in Applied
  Mathematics). Averaging and homogenization for ODEs, SDEs, and PDEs,
  treated first formally and then rigorously.
* **Graeme W. Milton, *The Theory of Composites*** (2002, [Cambridge
  University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511613357)). Effective
  moduli, bounds, and microstructures. Reissued by SIAM in 2022.
* **Luigi Ambrosio, Nicola Fusco, and Diego Pallara, *Functions of
  Bounded Variation and Free Discontinuity Problems***. The
  variational theory of BV and SBV energies.
  Also listed in Section 37.
* **Mariano Giaquinta, Giuseppe Modica, and Jiří Souček, *Cartesian
  Currents in the Calculus of Variations I–II***. Currents and weak
  convergence for variational problems involving mappings.
* **Jürgen Jost and Xianqing Li-Jost, *Calculus of Variations***. A
  geometric-analysis-oriented introduction.
* **Pablo Pedregal, *Parametrized Measures and Variational
  Principles***. The standard short account of Young measures.
* **Etienne Sandier and Sylvia Serfaty, *Vortices in the Magnetic
  Ginzburg–Landau Model*** (2007, [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-8176-4550-2)). The research monograph
  on vortex concentration in Ginzburg–Landau.
* **Gabriella Tarantello, *Selfdual Gauge Field Vortices: An Analytical
  Approach*** (2008, [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-8176-4608-0)). Self-dual vortices and the associated
  elliptic systems.
* **Antoine Henrot and Michel Pierre, *Shape Variation and
  Optimization: A Geometrical Analysis*** (2018, [European Mathematical
  Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/178); French original 2005). Shape derivatives and existence in shape optimisation.
  Also listed in Section 53.
* **Ivar Ekeland, *Convexity Methods in Hamiltonian Mechanics***.
  Convex duality and variational methods for periodic solutions of
  Hamiltonian systems.
* **N. S. Bakhvalov and G. P. Panasenko, *Homogenisation: Averaging
  Processes in Periodic Media***. Asymptotic expansions for periodic
  homogenization problems.
* **Jürgen Moser, *Selected Chapters in the Calculus of
  Variations***. Weierstrass fields, minimal geodesics, and Aubry–Mather
  theory.

### More recent works

* **Filip Rindler, *Calculus of Variations*** (2018, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-77637-8)). A
  modern graduate course: lower semicontinuity, Young measures,
  rigidity, and BV.
* **Hansjörg Kielhöfer, *Calculus of Variations: An Introduction to the
  One-Dimensional Theory with Examples and Exercises*** (2018,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-71123-2)). Classical
  variational calculus, second variations, and existence in one dimension. A
  focused graduate introduction that develops the foundational examples in
  detail.
* **Daniel Liberzon, *Calculus of Variations and Optimal Control Theory: A
  Concise Introduction*** (2012, [Princeton University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1515/9781400842643)). From the Euler–Lagrange
  equation to the maximum principle and dynamic programming.
* **Frank H. Clarke, *Functional Analysis, Calculus of Variations and
  Optimal Control*** (2013, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4471-4820-3)). Listed in full in Section 12.
* **Andrea Braides, *Local Minimization, Variational Evolution and
  Γ-Convergence*** (2014, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-01982-6), Lecture Notes in Mathematics).
  Variational evolution beyond global Γ-convergence.
* **Alexander Mielke and Tomáš Roubíček, *Rate-Independent Systems: Theory
  and Application*** (2015,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4939-2706-7)). Energetic
  solutions, evolutionary Γ-convergence, and applications to plasticity,
  damage, and fracture.
* **Zhongwei Shen, *Periodic Homogenization of Elliptic Systems*** (2018,
  [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-91214-1)). Convergence
  rates and uniform regularity estimates in periodic homogenization, in the
  line of Avellaneda–Lin and Kenig–Lin–Shen.
* **Scott Armstrong, Tuomo Kuusi, and Jean-Christophe Mourrat,
  *Quantitative Stochastic Homogenization and Large-Scale
  Regularity*** (2019, Springer). Quantitative stochastic homogenization for
  elliptic equations, including large-scale regularity and estimates for
  effective behaviour.
* **Sylvia Serfaty, *Coulomb Gases and Ginzburg–Landau Vortices***
  (2015, [European Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/152), Zurich Lectures in Advanced
  Mathematics). Mean-field limits, Coulomb gases, and the
  Ginzburg–Landau energy, in a research course.
  Also listed in Section 14.
* **Marco Mazzucchelli, *Critical Point Theory for Lagrangian Systems*** (2012,
  [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-0348-0163-8)). The Maslov index, Morse theory,
  and multiplicity of periodic orbits for Lagrangian systems.

---

<a id="ckma-37"></a>

## 37. Geometric Measure Theory, BV, Rectifiability, and Currents

Hausdorff measure, rectifiability, currents, varifolds, BV functions, and
sets of finite perimeter describe nonsmooth geometric objects and
integration over them. Fractal dimension and the geometry of exceptional
sets are included as part of this measure-theoretic viewpoint. Section 5
treats fine properties of functions, Section 36 variational applications,
and Section 52 minimal surfaces and geometric PDE. Section 39A includes
metric and fractal geometry, while 39B develops energies and diffusion on
nonsmooth spaces.

### Foundational works

* **Herbert Federer, *Geometric Measure Theory***. A research treatise on
  Hausdorff measure, rectifiability, currents, and the area and coarea
  formulas.
  Also listed in Section 5.
* **Leon Simon, *Lectures on Geometric Measure Theory***. An introduction to
  varifolds, monotonicity, compactness, and regularity, with applications to
  minimal surfaces.
* **Pertti Mattila, *Geometry of Sets and Measures in Euclidean
  Spaces***. The analyst's GMT, developed without current theory.
  Also listed in Section 5.
* **Luigi Ambrosio, Nicola Fusco, and Diego Pallara, *Functions of
  Bounded Variation and Free Discontinuity Problems***. A research reference
  on BV and SBV functions, lower semicontinuity, and free-discontinuity
  variational problems.
  Also listed in Section 36.
* **Lawrence C. Evans and Ronald F. Gariepy, *Measure Theory and Fine
  Properties of Functions***. A compact reference on differentiation of
  measures and the fine properties of Sobolev and BV functions.
  Also listed in Sections 5 and 26.
* **Enrico Giusti, *Minimal Surfaces and Functions of Bounded
  Variation***. The classical route from BV to perimeter minimization.
* **Frank Morgan, *Geometric Measure Theory: A Beginner's Guide***. A
  short, visual entry point before Simon or Federer.
* **Steven G. Krantz and Harold R. Parks, *Geometric Integration
  Theory***. Currents, area and coarea, and Plateau-type problems in a
  gentler presentation.
* **Fanghua Lin and Xiaoping Yang, *Geometric Measure Theory: An
  Introduction***. A modern introduction with geometric-analysis
  applications.
* **Camillo De Lellis, *Rectifiable Sets, Densities and Tangent
  Measures***. Research-level notes on modern rectifiability.
* **Guy David and Stephen Semmes, *Analysis of and on Uniformly
  Rectifiable Sets*** and ***Singular Integrals and Rectifiable Sets
  in R^n***. The quantitative rectifiability programme and its link
  with singular integrals.
* **Guy David, *Singular Sets of Minimizers for the Mumford–Shah
  Functional*** (2005, [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/b137039)).
  Regularity of the discontinuity set of Mumford–Shah minimizers, using
  uniform rectifiability.
* **Kenneth Falconer, *The Geometry of Fractal Sets*** and ***Fractal
  Geometry: Mathematical Foundations and Applications***. Hausdorff
  dimension, fractal sets, and the measure-theoretic techniques used to
  study them.
* **Hassler Whitney, *Geometric Integration Theory***. Geometric integration
  through chains, cochains, differential forms, and Lipschitz mappings,
  including the flat-norm approach to nonsmooth domains of integration.
* **C. A. Rogers, *Hausdorff Measures***. Listed in full in Section 5.
  The classical account of Hausdorff measure.
* **Richard J. Gardner, *Geometric Tomography***. Tomography of convex
  bodies and star bodies: projections, sections, and the determination of
  a body by its integral-geometric data.
  Also listed in Section 11.
* **Luis A. Santaló, *Integral Geometry and Geometric Probability***. The
  classical treatise on kinematic formulas, Crofton formulas, and
  geometric probability.
* **Rolf Schneider and Wolfgang Weil, *Stochastic and Integral
  Geometry***. Listed in full in Section 6.
* **Georges Matheron, *Random Sets and Integral Geometry***. Listed in full in Section 6.
* **Alexander Markoe, *Analytic Tomography***. The Radon transform and its inversion, from
  the analytic side of tomography. Distinct from Gardner's *Geometric Tomography*.

### More recent works

* **Francesco Maggi, *Sets of Finite Perimeter and Geometric
  Variational Problems*** (2012, Cambridge University Press). A graduate
  treatment of sets of finite perimeter, compactness, isoperimetric
  inequalities, and regularity of perimeter minimizers.
  Also listed in Section 14.
* **Pertti Mattila, *Rectifiability: A Survey*** (2023, [Cambridge
  University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781009288057), London Mathematical Society Lecture Note Series).
  A research survey of rectifiable sets and measures, currents, and
  varifolds, with applications to harmonic analysis, free boundaries, and
  geometric flows.
  Also listed in Section 5.
* **Xavier Tolsa, *Analytic Capacity, the Cauchy Transform, and
  Non-homogeneous Calderón–Zygmund Theory*** (2014, Birkhäuser). Analytic
  capacity, Cauchy transforms, removable sets, and semiadditivity of
  analytic capacity, using non-homogeneous Calderón–Zygmund theory.
* **Pertti Mattila, *Fourier Analysis and Hausdorff
  Dimension*** (2015, Cambridge University Press). Projections, intersections, and the Fourier-analytic
  methods.
  Also listed in Section 27.
* **Christopher J. Bishop and Yuval Peres, *Fractals in Probability
  and Analysis*** (2017, Cambridge University Press). A modern synthesis of dimension, Brownian motion,
  and analytic techniques.
* **Washek F. Pfeffer, *The Divergence Theorem and Sets of Finite
  Perimeter*** (2012, [CRC Press](https://doi.org/10.1201/b11919)). Listed in full in Section 5.
* **Bozhidar Velichkov, *Regularity of the One-phase Free Boundaries***
  (2023, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-13238-4)). Listed in full in Section 33. Free boundaries as a GMT
  problem.
* **William H. Meeks III and Joaquín Pérez, *A Survey on Classical
  Minimal Surface Theory*** (2012, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/ulect/060),
  University Lecture Series). Listed in full in Section 52.
* **Luigi Ambrosio (ed.), *Geometric Measure Theory and Real Analysis***
  (2014, [Edizioni della
  Normale/Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-88-7642-523-3)). Substantial
  lecture courses on infinite-dimensional Sobolev analysis, Heisenberg
  geometry, and regularity of minimal currents. A research-school volume
  rather than a general GMT textbook.

---

<a id="ckma-38"></a>

## 38. Potential Theory and Capacity

Harmonic, subharmonic, and superharmonic functions, capacities, Green
functions, and the Dirichlet problem form the core of potential theory.
Classical, axiomatic, nonlinear, and probabilistic treatments are included,
together with applications to approximation, boundary behaviour, and energy
minimization. Section 8 supplies the Markov-process viewpoint, Section 33
elliptic regularity, and Section 39B Dirichlet forms and heat kernels.
Complex logarithmic potential theory also connects with conformal mapping
and weighted approximation in Sections 43 and 47–48.

### Foundational works

* **Lester L. Helms, *Potential Theory***. A standard graduate route
  through harmonic, subharmonic, and superharmonic functions and the
  Dirichlet problem.
* **David H. Armitage and Stephen J. Gardiner, *Classical Potential
  Theory***. A detailed treatment of harmonic, subharmonic, and
  superharmonic functions, Green functions, and boundary behaviour.
* **Sheldon Axler, Paul Bourdon, and Wade Ramey, *Harmonic Function
  Theory***. A graduate treatment of harmonic functions on Euclidean spaces,
  including Poisson kernels, boundary behaviour, and harmonic function
  spaces.
* **N. S. Landkof, *Foundations of Modern Potential Theory***. The
  classical research monograph on Riesz kernels, capacities, and
  energy.
* **Joseph L. Doob, *Classical Potential Theory and Its Probabilistic
  Counterpart***. A deep and demanding synthesis of potential theory
  and Markov processes.
* **Marcel Brelot, *Éléments de la théorie classique du potentiel***
  and ***Lectures on Potential Theory***. The French axiomatic
  tradition.
* **Sidney C. Port and Charles J. Stone, *Brownian Motion and
  Classical Potential Theory***. The probabilistic treatment.
  Also listed in Section 8.
* **Richard F. Bass, *Probabilistic Techniques in Analysis***. Listed in full in Section 8. Brownian motion applied to harmonic functions and
  boundary behaviour.
* **Krzysztof Bogdan, Tomasz Byczkowski, Tadeusz Kulczycki, Michał Ryznar,
  Renming Song, and Zoran Vondraček, *Potential Analysis of Stable Processes
  and its Extensions*** (2009,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-02141-1), Lecture Notes in
  Mathematics). Potential theory of the fractional Laplacian: the boundary
  Harnack principle, Green functions, and Martin boundaries.
* **David R. Adams and Lars Inge Hedberg, *Function Spaces and
  Potential Theory***. Nonlinear capacities tied to Sobolev and Besov
  spaces.
  Also listed in Section 26.
* **Juha Heinonen, Tero Kilpeläinen, and Olli Martio, *Nonlinear
  Potential Theory of Degenerate Elliptic Equations***. The standard
  nonlinear reference.
  Also listed in Section 33.
* **W. K. Hayman and P. B. Kennedy, *Subharmonic Functions I***, and
  **W. K. Hayman, *Subharmonic Functions II***. The large classical reference on subharmonicity.
* **Thomas Ransford, *Potential Theory in the Complex
  Plane***. Concise, modern, and the standard reference for complex
  analysts.
* **Edward B. Saff and Vilmos Totik, *Logarithmic Potentials with
  External Fields***. Weighted potential theory, equilibrium measures,
  and the link to orthogonal polynomials and random matrices.
  Also listed in Section 47.
* **M. Tsuji, *Potential Theory in Modern Function Theory***. The
  classical bridge to one-variable complex analysis.
* **Luca Capogna, Carlos E. Kenig, and Loredana Lanzani, *Harmonic
  Measure*** (2005, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/ulect/035)). Listed in full in Section 25.
* **Masatoshi Fukushima, Yoichi Oshima, and Masayoshi Takeda,
  *Dirichlet Forms and Symmetric Markov Processes***. Potential theory
  in its modern functional-analytic form; see also Section 39.
  Also listed in Section 39.
* **Oliver Dimon Kellogg, *Foundations of Potential Theory***. The
  classical introduction to Newtonian potentials, harmonic functions, and
  the Dirichlet problem.
* **Stephen J. Gardiner, *Harmonic Approximation***. Runge-type and
  Arakelian-type approximation by harmonic functions.
* **Ross G. Pinsky, *Positive Harmonic Functions and Diffusion***. The
  analytic and probabilistic theory of positive harmonic functions,
  criticality, and Martin boundaries.
* **Christian Berg and Gunnar Forst, *Potential Theory on Locally Compact Abelian Groups***.
  Potential kernels, convolution semigroups, and potential theory on locally compact abelian
  groups. Also listed in Section 28.

### More recent works

* **Anders Björn and Jana Björn, *Nonlinear Potential Theory on Metric
  Spaces*** (2011, [European Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/099)). Newtonian spaces, capacities, and p-harmonic functions
  beyond Euclidean space.
  Also listed in Section 39.
* **Manfred Stoll, *Harmonic and Subharmonic Function Theory on the Hyperbolic
  Ball*** (2016, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9781316341063)). Invariant harmonic
  functions, potentials, and boundary questions on hyperbolic space. The
  non-Euclidean counterpart of classical harmonic-function theory.
* **Augusto C. Ponce, *Elliptic PDEs, Measures and Capacities*** (2016,
  [European Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/140)). Listed in full in Section 26. Nonlinear
  capacities and measure data.
* **Dorina Mitrea, Irina Mitrea, Marius Mitrea, and Michael Taylor,
  *The Hodge-Laplacian*** (2016, [De Gruyter](https://doi.org/10.1515/9783110484380)).
  Listed in full in Section 25. Layer potentials and boundary-value problems for the
  Hodge Laplacian on Lipschitz domains in Riemannian manifolds.
* **N. Th. Varopoulos, *Potential Theory and Geometry on Lie Groups*** (2020,
  [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781139567718)).
  Potential theory, diffusion, and the geometry of Lie groups. A specialised
  synthesis linking probabilistic and analytic techniques with large-scale
  Lie-group geometry.
* **Anton Bovier and Frank den Hollander, *Metastability: A
  Potential-Theoretic Approach*** (2015,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-24777-9)). Rare transitions,
  capacities, and sharp transition-time asymptotics for stochastic systems. A
  major research synthesis showing potential theory at work in probability.
* **Sergiy V. Borodachov, Douglas P. Hardin, and Edward B. Saff, *Discrete
  Energy on Rectifiable Sets*** (2019,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-387-84808-2)). Minimal Riesz
  energy, best packing, and the asymptotic distribution of point
  configurations.

---

<a id="ckma-39"></a>

## 39. Metric Measure Spaces, Dirichlet Forms, Heat Kernels, Optimal Transport, and Fractals

Analysis specified by distances, measures, energies, and transport is
divided into metric measure spaces and fractal geometry (39A), Dirichlet
forms and heat kernels with graph and fractal diffusion (39B), and optimal
transport and metric gradient flows (39C). These structures interact without
being interchangeable: neither a diffusion nor a transport regularity theory
follows from a metric measure space alone. Sections 14, 26, 37, and 38
provide complementary inequality, function-space, geometric-measure, and
potential-theoretic viewpoints.

<a id="ckma-39a"></a>

### 39A. Metric Measure Spaces and Fractal Geometry

Upper gradients, metric Sobolev spaces, quasiconformal geometry, metric
convergence, and fractal dimensions develop analysis and geometry without
fixed smooth coordinates.

#### Foundational works

* **Juha Heinonen, *Lectures on Analysis on Metric Spaces***. The
  standard introduction to first-order analysis without smooth
  structure.

* **Luigi Ambrosio and Paolo Tilli, *Topics on Analysis in Metric
  Spaces***. Hausdorff measures, Lipschitz maps, and Gromov–Hausdorff
  convergence.

* **Dmitri Burago, Yuri Burago, and Sergei Ivanov, *A Course in Metric
  Geometry***. The geometric background that metric analysis assumes.

* **Mikhail Gromov, *Metric Structures for Riemannian and
  Non-Riemannian Spaces***. Metric geometry, convergence of spaces, and
  geometric inequalities, with applications to Riemannian and nonsmooth
  spaces.

* **Kenneth Falconer, *Fractal Geometry: Mathematical Foundations and
  Applications*** (3rd ed., 2014, [Wiley](https://www.wiley-vch.de/de/fachgebiete/mathematik-und-statistik/fractal-geometry-978-1-119-94239-9)). Hausdorff and box dimensions,
  projections, self-similar sets, and dynamical examples. The standard
  graduate introduction.

#### More recent works

* **Juha Heinonen, Pekka Koskela, Nageswari Shanmugalingam, and Jeremy
  T. Tyson, *Sobolev Spaces on Metric Measure Spaces*** (2015, Cambridge University Press). The
  comprehensive reference for upper gradients, Poincaré inequalities,
  and Newtonian spaces.

* **Anders Björn and Jana Björn, *Nonlinear Potential Theory on Metric
  Spaces*** (2011, [European Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/099)). Capacity and p-harmonic theory in doubling and Poincaré
  spaces.
  Also listed in Section 38.

* **John M. Mackay and Jeremy T. Tyson, *Conformal Dimension: Theory and
  Application*** (2010, [American Mathematical
  Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/ulect/054), University Lecture Series).
  The conformal dimension of metric spaces and its uses in geometric group
  theory and dynamics.

* **Miroslav Bačák, *Convex Analysis and Optimization in Hadamard Spaces***
  (2014, [De Gruyter](https://doi.org/10.1515/9783110361629)). Convex
  functions, proximal methods, and gradient flows in spaces of nonpositive
  curvature. Extends Hilbert-space variational methods to nonlinear metric
  settings.

* **Jun Kigami, *Geometry and Analysis of Metric Spaces via Weighted
  Partitions*** (2020, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-54154-5)).
  Weighted partitions link compact metric spaces with infinite graphs,
  energies, and conformal dimension. A research continuation of Kigami's
  resistance-form and fractal-analysis books.

* **Laurent Younes, *Shapes and Diffeomorphisms*** (2nd ed., 2019,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-662-58496-5)). Shape spaces of curves and
  surfaces, and the diffeomorphic metrics used to compare them. The first edition appeared
  in 2010.

* **Balázs Bárány, Károly Simon, and Boris Solomyak, *Self-similar and
  Self-affine Sets and Measures*** (2023, [American Mathematical
  Society](https://www.ams.org/books/surv/276/)). Dimensions, measures,
  overlaps, and projections for iterated function systems. A substantial
  research synthesis connecting geometric measure theory, fractals, and
  dynamical methods.

* **Jonathan M. Fraser, *Assouad Dimension and Fractal Geometry*** (2020,
  [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781108778459)).
  Assouad and related dimensions, with applications to embeddings and to
  dynamically and arithmetically defined sets.

<a id="ckma-39b"></a>

### 39B. Dirichlet Forms, Heat Kernels, and Analysis on Graphs and Fractals

Closed energy forms, Markov semigroups, heat kernels, resistance forms, and
spectral methods describe diffusion on manifolds, graphs, and fractals.

#### Foundational works

* **Masatoshi Fukushima, Yoichi Oshima, and Masayoshi Takeda,
  *Dirichlet Forms and Symmetric Markov Processes***. The standard
  reference connecting forms, processes, and potential theory.
  Also listed in Section 38.

* **Alexander Grigor'yan, *Heat Kernel and Analysis on
  Manifolds***. Heat kernels, volume growth, and parabolic Harnack
  inequalities.
  Also listed in Sections 14 and 52.

* **E. Brian Davies, *Heat Kernels and Spectral Theory***. Semigroup and spectral
  methods for elliptic operators, including ultracontractivity and
  heat-kernel estimates.

* **Laurent Saloff-Coste, *Aspects of Sobolev-Type
  Inequalities***. Sobolev, Nash, and Poincaré inequalities on
  manifolds and graphs.
  Also listed in Section 14.

* **Jun Kigami, *Analysis on Fractals***. The foundational monograph
  on Dirichlet forms and Laplacians on self-similar sets.

* **Robert S. Strichartz, *Differential Equations on Fractals: A
  Tutorial***. The readable introduction to the same subject.

* **Martin T. Barlow, *Diffusions on Fractals*** (1998, lecture course in
  *Lectures on Probability Theory and Statistics*,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/BFb0092537), Lecture Notes in Mathematics
  1690, pp. 1–121). Random walks, diffusion processes, potential theory,
  and heat-kernel estimates on fractals. His *Random Walks and Heat
  Kernels on Graphs* is listed among the more recent works.

* **Michel L. Lapidus and Machiel van Frankenhuijsen, *Fractal
  Geometry, Complex Dimensions and Zeta Functions: Geometry and Spectra
  of Fractal Strings*** (2nd ed., 2013, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4614-2176-4)). Fractal strings and
  complex dimensions.

#### More recent works

* **Martin T. Barlow, *Random Walks and Heat Kernels on Graphs*** (2017,
  [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781107415690),
  London Mathematical Society Lecture Note Series). Random walks and
  electrical networks, isoperimetric and Poincaré inequalities, and
  Gaussian and sub-Gaussian heat-kernel bounds on graphs.
  Also listed in Section 8.

* **Zhen-Qing Chen and Masatoshi Fukushima, *Symmetric Markov
  Processes, Time Change, and Boundary Theory*** (2012, Princeton University Press).
  Symmetric Markov processes and Dirichlet forms, with detailed treatments
  of time changes and boundary theory.

* **Dominique Bakry, Ivan Gentil, and Michel Ledoux, *Analysis and
  Geometry of Markov Diffusion Operators*** (2014, Springer). The central modern
  reference for Γ-calculus and curvature–dimension conditions.
  Also listed in Section 14.

* **Alexander Grigor'yan, *Introduction to Analysis on Graphs*** (2018,
  [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/ulect/071),
  University Lecture Series). Laplacians on graphs, random walks, and
  spectral and isoperimetric estimates.

* **Matthias Keller, Daniel Lenz, and Radosław K. Wojciechowski, *Graphs and
  Discrete Dirichlet Spaces*** (2021,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-81459-5)). Dirichlet forms,
  heat kernels, spectra, and geometry on weighted graphs. A substantial
  analytic reference where discreteness makes the general ideas particularly
  concrete.

* **Takashi Kumagai, *Random Walks on Disordered Media and their Scaling
  Limits*** (2014, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-03152-1), Lecture Notes in Mathematics). Heat
  kernels and scaling limits on fractals and random media, from the
  Saint-Flour lectures.

* **Jun Kigami, *Resistance Forms, Quasisymmetric Maps and Heat Kernel
  Estimates*** (2012, Memoirs of the [American Mathematical Society](https://bookstore.ams.org/memo-216-1015/)). A
  research monograph on resistance forms beyond the post-critically
  finite sets of his earlier book.

* **Michel L. Lapidus, Goran Radunović, and Darko Žubrinić, *Fractal
  Zeta Functions and Fractal Drums: Higher-Dimensional Theory of
  Complex Dimensions*** (2017, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-44706-3)). The higher-dimensional
  extension of the fractal-string theory.

<a id="ckma-39c"></a>

### 39C. Optimal Transport, Wasserstein Geometry, and Metric Gradient Flows

Transport plans and maps, duality, Wasserstein distances, gradient flows,
and curvature-dimension methods connect optimization, PDE, and metric
geometry.

#### Foundational works

* **Cédric Villani, *Topics in Optimal Transportation***. A concise
  route to Kantorovich duality, displacement interpolation, and
  transport inequalities.

* **Cédric Villani, *Optimal Transport: Old and New***. The
  comprehensive reference connecting transport with PDE, geometry,
  probability, and entropy.
  Also listed in Section 14.

* **Luigi Ambrosio, Nicola Gigli, and Giuseppe Savaré, *Gradient Flows
  in Metric Spaces and in the Space of Probability Measures***. The
  foundational metric theory of gradient flows and Wasserstein
  evolution.

#### More recent works

* **Filippo Santambrogio, *Optimal Transport for Applied
  Mathematicians*** (2015, Birkhäuser). Kantorovich duality, Wasserstein
  distances, and transport maps, developed toward PDE and variational
  applications.

* **Alessio Figalli and Federico Glaudo, *An Invitation to Optimal
  Transport, Wasserstein Distances, and Gradient Flows*** (2021, European Mathematical Society).
  A short introduction to optimal transport, including transport maps,
  Wasserstein geometry, and applications.

* **Luigi Ambrosio, Elia Brué, and Daniele Semola, *Lectures on
  Optimal Transport*** (2021, Springer). A graduate course connecting
  optimal transport with analysis on metric measure spaces.

* **Luigi Ambrosio and Nicola Gigli, *A User's Guide to Optimal Transport***
  (2013, chapter in *Modelling and Optimisation of Flows on Networks*,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-32160-3_1), Lecture Notes in
  Mathematics 2062, pp. 1–155). Kantorovich duality, Wasserstein geometry,
  gradient flows, and curvature-dimension conditions, in long lecture notes.

* **D. J. H. Garling, *Analysis on Polish Spaces and an Introduction to
  Optimal Transportation*** (2018, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781108377362)). Measures on Polish spaces,
  weak convergence, and transport developed together. A graduate bridge from
  abstract measure theory to Kantorovich's theory.

* **Nicola Gigli and Enrico Pasqualetto, *Lectures on Nonsmooth
  Differential Geometry*** (2020, Springer). Differential calculus on metric measure
  spaces and the RCD theory.

* **Alessio Figalli, *The Monge–Ampère Equation and Its
  Applications*** (2017, European Mathematical Society). Transport regularity through Monge–Ampère theory.
  Also listed in Section 33.

* **Ludovic Rifford, *Sub-Riemannian Geometry and Optimal Transport***
  (2014, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-04804-8)). A short bridge between sub-Riemannian geometry and
  optimal transport. The full sub-Riemannian treatise is in Section 52.

* **Gabriel Peyré and Marco Cuturi, *Computational Optimal
  Transport*** (2019, Now Publishers). Computational in emphasis, but the standard reference
  for entropic regularization, which has analytic content of its own.


---

# Part VII. Complex Analysis

The literatures are distinct: the graduate courses in one variable,
the classical function theory of entire and meromorphic functions, the
analytic function spaces and their operators, geometric function
theory, and the several-variable theory. Riemann surfaces and complex
dynamics follow.

---

<a id="ckma-40"></a>

## 40. One Complex Variable: General Theory

Holomorphic functions of one complex variable are developed through
integration, power series, residues, normal families, conformal mapping,
analytic continuation, and classical examples. Broad courses and treatises
provide the general theory, including routes toward Riemann surfaces and
special functions. Sections 41–44 develop growth and value distribution,
analytic function spaces, conformal extremal theory, and surfaces and
quasiconformal mappings. Section 45 treats several variables and Section 46
iteration, while Sections 49–51 develop special-function and asymptotic
applications.

### Foundational works

* **Lars V. Ahlfors, *Complex Analysis***. A first graduate course with a
  geometric emphasis, covering holomorphic functions, conformal mapping, and
  classical function theory.
* **John B. Conway, *Functions of One Complex Variable I***. A systematic first
  graduate course in holomorphic functions, integration, and classical
  complex function theory.
* **John B. Conway, *Functions of One Complex Variable II***. Hardy
  spaces, harmonic functions, potential theory, and analytic
  continuation at second-course level.
* **Elias M. Stein and Rami Shakarchi, *Complex
  Analysis***. Especially effective at connecting complex analysis
  with Fourier analysis and analytic number theory.
* **Theodore W. Gamelin, *Complex Analysis***. A modern one-volume
  course with a strong function-theoretic viewpoint and excellent
  exercises.
* **Walter Rudin, *Real and Complex Analysis***. A compressed
  measure-theoretic development of holomorphic functions, Hardy
  spaces, and boundary behaviour.
  Also listed in Sections 1, 4, and 54.
* **Serge Lang, *Complex Analysis***. Broad and classical, with
  elliptic functions and analytic continuation.
* **Reinhold Remmert, *Theory of Complex Functions***. A detailed treatment of
  classical complex function theory, with historical commentary and careful
  discussion of the basic concepts.
  Also listed in Section 2.
* **Raghavan Narasimhan and Yves Nievergelt, *Complex Analysis in One
  Variable***. Concise, geometric, rigorous, and with an unusually
  good problem section.
* **David C. Ullrich, *Complex Made Simple*** (2008, [American
  Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/097), Graduate Studies in Mathematics).
  A concise graduate course with detailed explanations of the main theorems
  and their proofs.
* **Eberhard Freitag and Rolf Busam, *Complex Analysis*** (2nd ed., 2009,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-540-93983-2), Universitext). One
  complex variable through elliptic functions, modular forms, and analytic
  number theory.
* **Rubí E. Rodríguez, Irwin Kra, and Jane P. Gilman, *Complex Analysis: In
  the Spirit of Lipman Bers*** (2nd ed., 2013,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4419-7323-8), Graduate Texts in
  Mathematics). A first graduate course leading towards Riemann surfaces and
  Fuchsian groups.
* **Robert B. Burckel, *An Introduction to Classical Complex
  Analysis***. An extensive reference on classical one-variable complex
  analysis, with careful statements of hypotheses and detailed attribution.
  Its 2021 successor, *Classical Analysis in the Complex Plane*, is listed
  among the more recent works.
* **Maurice Heins, *Complex Function Theory***. Idiosyncratic, deep,
  and strong on the potential-theoretic and boundary aspects.
* **Robert E. Greene and Steven G. Krantz, *Function Theory of One
  Complex Variable***. A thorough modern graduate text with a view
  toward several variables.
* **Bruce P. Palka, *An Introduction to Complex Function Theory***. A
  leisurely and thorough alternative at the same level.
* **Eberhard Freitag, *Complex Analysis 2: Riemann Surfaces, Several Complex
  Variables, Abelian Functions, Higher Modular Functions*** (German
  original 2009; English translation 2011, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-20554-5)). A broad second
  course moving from Riemann surfaces to several variables and abelian
  functions. Included for its complex-analytic treatment of material often
  approached algebraically.
* **A. I. Markushevich, *Theory of Functions of a Complex Variable***
  (3 volumes). A comprehensive classical course in one-variable complex
  analysis, extending from elementary theory to advanced function theory.
* **E. C. Titchmarsh, *The Theory of Functions***. A classical
  treatise covering both real and complex function theory, with
  material hard to find elsewhere.
* **Saks and Zygmund, *Analytic Functions***. A classical
  Polish-school text of enduring precision.
* **Steven G. Krantz, *Handbook of Complex Variables*** and ***Complex
  Analysis: The Geometric Viewpoint***. A formula reference and an
  essay-like geometric treatment respectively.
* **Kunihiko Kodaira, *Complex Analysis***. A geometric course leading
  to Riemann surfaces and the uniformization theorem.

### More recent works

* **Robert B. Burckel, *Classical Analysis in the Complex Plane*** (2021,
  [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-0716-1965-0)). A revised and
  expanded successor to his 1979 *Introduction to Classical Complex
  Analysis*, in the same exhaustive style.
* **Wilhelm Schlag, *A Course in Complex Analysis and Riemann Surfaces***
  (2014, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/154), Graduate Studies in Mathematics).
  A one-year course from the Cauchy theorem to Riemann surfaces and a
  first look at Riemann–Hilbert problems.
  Also listed in Section 44.
* **Michael E. Taylor, *Introduction to Complex Analysis*** (2019, [American
  Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/202), Graduate Studies
  in Mathematics). Complex analysis with Fourier analysis, special
  functions, and Riemann surfaces brought in early.
* **Serge Lvovski, *Principles of Complex Analysis*** (2020,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-59365-0)). A substantial course
  including classical function theory and the geometric viewpoint. Useful
  beside the shorter first-course texts for a more extended development.
* **Barry Simon, *Basic Complex Analysis*** and ***Advanced Complex
  Analysis*** (Parts 2A and 2B of *A Comprehensive Course in
  Analysis*; 2015, American Mathematical Society). Wide coverage, extensive problems, and reliable
  historical notes.
* **Ranjan Roy, *Sources in the Development of Mathematics*** (2011,
  [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511844195)) and his *Elliptic and Modular Functions
  from Gauss to Dedekind to Hecke* (2017, Cambridge University Press).
  Listed in full in Section 2. Source-driven accounts that double as classical
  reading.

---

<a id="ckma-41"></a>

## 41. Classical Function Theory: Entire and Meromorphic Functions, Value Distribution

Entire and meromorphic functions are studied through growth, zeros,
factorization, value distribution, and uniqueness. Completeness of
exponential systems, quasi-analyticity, and complex differential equations
link classical function theory with harmonic analysis and asymptotics.
Section 40 supplies the general complex theory, Section 42 analytic Hilbert
and Banach spaces, and Section 46 complex dynamics. Beurling–Malliavin and
uncertainty methods also connect with Section 27; Painlevé and related
equations with Sections 50–51.

### Foundational works

* **Ralph P. Boas, Jr., *Entire Functions***. The standard reference
  for growth, order and type, indicator functions, and completeness
  theorems.
* **Louis de Branges, *Hilbert Spaces of Entire Functions***. Hilbert
  spaces of entire functions, organised by a nested chain of subspaces.
  The source of the function-theoretic model behind de Branges–Rovnyak
  spaces. Also listed in Section 42.
* **B. Ja. Levin, *Distribution of Zeros of Entire Functions***. A research
  monograph on zero distribution, Hadamard factorization, and functions of
  completely regular growth.
* **B. Ja. Levin, *Lectures on Entire Functions***. A more readable
  presentation of the same material.
* **Rolf Nevanlinna, *Analytic Functions***. The classical formulation
  of value-distribution theory by its founder.
* **W. K. Hayman, *Meromorphic Functions***. The English-school
  research monograph on Nevanlinna theory, deficiencies, and extremal
  problems.
* **A. A. Gol'dberg and I. V. Ostrovskii, *Value Distribution of
  Meromorphic Functions***. A detailed treatment of value distribution for
  meromorphic functions, including growth, deficiencies, and asymptotic
  behaviour.
* **Ilpo Laine, *Nevanlinna Theory and Complex Differential
  Equations***. The standard application of value distribution to
  differential equations in the complex plane.
* **Valerii I. Gromak, Ilpo Laine, and Shun Shimomura, *Painlevé
  Differential Equations in the Complex Plane*** (2002, [De Gruyter](https://doi.org/10.1515/9783110198096)).
  The complex-analytic theory of Painlevé equations, complementary to
  the Riemann–Hilbert treatise of Fokas, Its, Kapaev, and Novokshenov
  (Section 51).
  Also listed in Section 51.
* **Min Ru, *Nevanlinna Theory and Its Relation to Diophantine
  Approximation***. The Vojta dictionary, and the modern
  several-variable extensions.
* **William Cherry and Zhuan Ye, *Nevanlinna's Theory of Value
  Distribution***. A careful modern account with the error terms made
  precise.
* **Paul Koosis, *The Logarithmic Integral I–II***. Quasi-analyticity, weighted
  approximation, and Beurling–Malliavin theory developed through the
  logarithmic integral and related function-theoretic methods.
* **Paul Koosis, *Leçons sur le théorème de Beurling et
  Malliavin***. The focused account of the multiplier theorem.
* **Norman Levinson, *Gap and Density Theorems***. The classical
  monograph on completeness of exponentials.
* **R. E. A. C. Paley and Norbert Wiener, *Fourier Transforms in the
  Complex Domain***. The founding text of the Paley–Wiener circle of
  ideas.
* **Lars Hörmander, *Notions of Convexity***. Convexity, subharmonicity, and
  plurisubharmonicity, with applications to complex analysis. Also listed in Section 45 for its several-variable theory.
* **Carlos A. Berenstein and Roger Gay, *Complex Analysis and Special
  Topics in Harmonic Analysis*** and ***Complex Variables: An
  Introduction***. A large modern treatment linking entire functions,
  convolution equations, and harmonic analysis.
* **Lee A. Rubel with James E. Colliander, *Entire and Meromorphic
  Functions***. A short modern graduate treatment.
* **J. Ben Hough, Manjunath Krishnapur, Yuval Peres, and Bálint Virág,
  *Zeros of Gaussian Analytic Functions and Determinantal Point
  Processes*** (2009, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/ulect/051)). Gaussian analytic
  functions, their zero sets, and the determinantal processes around
  them.
* **Georges Valiron, *Lectures on the General Theory of Integral
  Functions***. A classical treatment of entire functions, their growth, and
  the distribution of their zeros.
* **T. Sheil-Small, *Complex Polynomials***. Zeros, critical points,
  and extremal problems for polynomials.

### More recent works

* **Jianhua Zheng, *Value Distribution of Meromorphic Functions*** (2010,
  [Springer and Tsinghua University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-12909-4)). Nevanlinna theory
  in angular domains, singular and T-directions, deficient values, growth,
  singularities of inverse functions, and Eremenko's proof of the
  Nevanlinna conjecture.
* **Norbert Steinmetz, *Nevanlinna Theory, Normal Families, and Algebraic
  Differential Equations*** (2017,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-59800-0)). Value distribution
  and normal families applied to differential equations in the complex plane.
  A modern synthesis connecting two classical strands of function theory.
* **Alexei Poltoratski, *Toeplitz Approach to Problems of the
  Uncertainty Principle*** (2015, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/cbms/121)). Listed in full in Section 21. A modern route into Beurling–Malliavin theory.
* **Nalini Joshi, *Discrete Painlevé Equations*** (2019, [American
  Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/cbms/131), CBMS). Listed in full in Section 51.
* **W. K. Hayman and Eleanor F. Lingham, *Research Problems in Function
  Theory*** (2019, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-25165-9)). The fiftieth-anniversary renewal of
  Hayman's problem list. Also listed in Section 54.

---

<a id="ckma-42"></a>

## 42. Hardy, Bergman, and Model Spaces; Operators on Analytic Function Spaces

Hardy, Bergman, Dirichlet, Fock, and model spaces are studied through
boundary values, kernels, interpolation, and the operators acting on them.
Toeplitz, Hankel, composition, and subnormal operators connect holomorphic
function theory with Hilbert-space methods and systems theory. Section 21
develops abstract operator models, Section 27 real-variable
harmonic-analysis counterparts, and Section 43 conformal boundary geometry.
Several-variable function spaces and Bergman-kernel geometry also connect
with Section 45.

### Foundational works

* **John B. Garnett, *Bounded Analytic Functions***. A research reference for
  H-infinity, BMO, Carleson measures, interpolating sequences, and the
  corona theorem.
* **Peter L. Duren, *Theory of H^p Spaces***. The standard first
  monograph on Hardy spaces in the disc.
* **Paul Koosis, *Introduction to H_p Spaces***. A complementary
  treatment on the line and the circle, with a strong classical
  flavour and much unusual material.
* **Kenneth Hoffman, *Banach Spaces of Analytic Functions***. A
  compact classic on H-infinity, the disc algebra, and the
  functional-analytic viewpoint.
* **Javad Mashreghi, *Representation Theorems in Hardy Spaces*** (2009,
  [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511814525)). Integral representations and the
  classical function theory of Hardy spaces, in textbook form.
* **Nikolai Nikolski, *Operators, Functions, and Systems
  I–II***. Hankel and Toeplitz operators, model spaces, and the
  systems-theoretic connection.
* **Joseph A. Cima, Alec L. Matheson, and William T. Ross, *The Cauchy
  Transform***. Cauchy transforms of measures and their boundary behaviour,
  with applications to analytic function spaces and operator theory.
* **Håkan Hedenmalm, Boris Korenblum, and Kehe Zhu, *Theory of Bergman
  Spaces***. The modern standard for Bergman spaces.
* **Peter L. Duren and Alexander Schuster, *Bergman Spaces*** (2004,
  [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/surv/100)). A complementary account to
  Hedenmalm–Korenblum–Zhu.
* **Kehe Zhu, *Spaces of Holomorphic Functions in the Unit Ball***
  (2005, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/0-387-27539-8), Graduate Texts in Mathematics). Holomorphic function
  spaces on the ball, the several-variable companion to the one-variable
  theory.
* **Kehe Zhu, *Operator Theory in Function Spaces***. Toeplitz, Hankel,
  and composition operators across the standard analytic function spaces.
  Also listed in Section 21.
* **Carl C. Cowen and Barbara D. MacCluer, *Composition Operators on
  Spaces of Analytic Functions***. The standard reference for
  composition operators.
* **Joel H. Shapiro, *Composition Operators and Classical Function
  Theory***. Composition operators studied through classical analytic
  function theory, with emphasis on compactness and the geometry of the
  inducing maps.
* **Albrecht Böttcher and Bernd Silbermann, *Analysis of Toeplitz
  Operators***. The large reference for Toeplitz operators and their
  symbols.
  Also listed in Section 21.
* **Vladimir V. Peller, *Hankel Operators and Their
  Applications***. A detailed treatment of Hankel operators, their symbols,
  Schatten-class properties, and applications to approximation and
  perturbation theory.
* **Victor Havin and Burglind Jöricke, *The Uncertainty Principle in
  Harmonic Analysis***. Quasi-analyticity, uniqueness theorems, and
  the complex-analytic side of uncertainty.
* **Marvin Rosenblum and James Rovnyak, *Hardy Classes and Operator
  Theory*** and ***Topics in Hardy Classes and Univalent
  Functions***. Function theory written for operator theorists.
  Also listed in Section 21.
* **Jim Agler and John E. McCarthy, *Pick Interpolation and Hilbert
  Function Spaces***. Interpolation, the Nevanlinna–Pick problem, and
  multivariable operator theory.
* **Louis de Branges, *Hilbert Spaces of Entire Functions***. Listed in full in Section 41. The classical source of the spaces from which
  de Branges–Rovnyak theory descends.
* **John B. Conway, *The Theory of Subnormal Operators***. Subnormal operators
  developed through spectral theory, measures, and spaces of analytic
  functions.
  Also listed in Section 21.

### More recent works

* **Nikolai Nikolski, *Hardy Spaces*** (2019, Cambridge University
  Press; French original 2012). A modern graduate course from the
  Russian school.
* **Stephan Ramon Garcia, Javad Mashreghi, and William T. Ross,
  *Introduction to Model Spaces and Their Operators*** (2016, Cambridge
  University Press). With Cima–Matheson–Ross (above), the modern
  accessible route into model space theory.
* **Vern I. Paulsen and Mrinal Raghupathi, *An Introduction to the Theory
  of Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Spaces*** (2016, Cambridge University
  Press). A graduate introduction to reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces,
  positive definite kernels, and their connections with interpolation and
  operator theory.
  Also listed in Section 21.
* **Kehe Zhu, *Analysis on Fock Spaces*** (2012, Springer, Graduate
  Texts in Mathematics). Toeplitz, Hankel, and related operators on
  the Fock space, the companion to *Operator Theory in Function Spaces*.
* **Omar El-Fallah, Karim Kellay, Javad Mashreghi, and Thomas Ransford,
  *A Primer on the Dirichlet Space*** (2014, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9781107239425)). The Dirichlet space, its capacity, and its operators, in a
  single graduate monograph.
* **Nicola Arcozzi, Richard Rochberg, Eric T. Sawyer, and Brett D. Wick, *The
  Dirichlet Space and Related Function Spaces*** (2019, [American Mathematical
  Society](https://www.ams.org/books/surv/239/)). Dirichlet spaces, Carleson
  measures, interpolation, and operator theory. A substantial research
  reference beyond the shorter primer.
* **Emmanuel Fricain and Javad Mashreghi, *The Theory of H(b) Spaces***
  (2 volumes, 2016, Cambridge University Press; [I](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9781139226752), [II](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9781139226769)). Listed in full in Section 21.
* **Stephan Ramon Garcia, Javad Mashreghi, and William T. Ross,
  *Operator Theory by Example*** (2023, [Oxford University Press](https://doi.org/10.1093/oso/9780192863867.001.0001)).
  Listed in full in Section 21.
* **Stephan Ramon Garcia, Javad Mashreghi, and William T. Ross, *Finite
