  Blaschke Products and Their Connections*** (2018, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-78247-8)).
  Blaschke products as the concrete core of inner-function theory.
* **Miroslav Pavlović, *Function Classes on the Unit Disc: An
  Introduction*** (2nd ed., 2019, [De
  Gruyter](https://doi.org/10.1515/9783110630855)). Hardy, Bergman, Bloch,
  Besov, and mixed-norm spaces, with coefficient multipliers.
* **Steven G. Krantz, *Geometric Analysis of the Bergman Kernel and Metric***
  (2013, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4614-7924-6)). Bergman
  kernels, invariant metrics, and the geometry of domains. A
  graduate-to-research continuation connecting analytic function spaces with
  several complex variables.
* **Kehe Zhu (ed.), *Handbook of Analytic Operator Theory*** (2019, [CRC
  Press](https://www.routledge.com/Handbook-of-Analytic-Operator-Theory/Zhu/p/book/9781138486416)). Listed in full in Section 21.
* **Alexei Poltoratski, *Toeplitz Approach to Problems of the
  Uncertainty Principle*** (2015, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/cbms/121)). Listed in full in Section 21.
* **Raymond Cheng, Javad Mashreghi, and William T. Ross, *Function Theory and
  ℓ^p Spaces*** (2020, [American Mathematical
  Society](https://www.ams.org/books/ulect/075/ulect075-endmatter.pdf)).
  Analytic functions studied through summability of their Taylor coefficients.
  A specialised account of coefficient spaces and their differences from
  classical Hardy spaces.

---

<a id="ckma-43"></a>

## 43. Univalent Functions, Conformal Mapping, and Extremal Methods

Univalent and multivalent functions, conformal maps, coefficient and
extremal problems, harmonic measure, extremal length, and symmetrization
describe planar analytic geometry. Boundary behaviour, circle packing, and
Loewner evolution connect the classical theory with discrete and
probabilistic conformal geometry. Section 40 supplies general complex
analysis, Section 38 potential theory, and Section 44 quasiconformal
deformation and surfaces. SLE and random conformal geometry are also treated
in Section 8, and several-variable extensions in Section 45.

### Foundational works

* **Peter L. Duren, *Univalent Functions***. The standard modern
  monograph on schlicht functions, coefficient problems, extremal
  methods, and the Bieberbach circle of ideas.
* **Christian Pommerenke, *Univalent Functions***. A complementary
  research reference, strong on boundary behaviour and Löwner theory.
* **Christian Pommerenke, *Boundary Behaviour of Conformal
  Maps***. The standard reference on prime ends, angular limits,
  harmonic measure, and the geometry of the boundary.
* **G. M. Goluzin, *Geometric Theory of Functions of a Complex
  Variable***. The large classical Russian source for extremal and
  variational methods.
* **Zeev Nehari, *Conformal Mapping***. A classical analytic text on
  conformal mapping and the associated extremal problems.
* **Lars V. Ahlfors, *Conformal Invariants: Topics in Geometric
  Function Theory***. Extremal length, harmonic measure, and conformal
  invariants in concentrated form.
* **James A. Jenkins, *Univalent Functions and Conformal
  Mapping***. The module and extremal-metric method.
* **Olli Lehto, *Univalent Functions and Teichmüller Spaces***. The
  bridge from univalence to quasiconformal deformation.
* **John B. Garnett and Donald E. Marshall, *Harmonic Measure***. A detailed
  reference for harmonic measure, extremal length, conformal mapping, and
  boundary geometry.
* **Ian Graham and Gabriela Kohr, *Geometric Function Theory in One and
  Higher Dimensions*** (2003, [CRC
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1201/9780203911624)). Univalent functions and
  Loewner theory in one variable and their extensions to the unit ball in
  C^n.
* **Reiner Kühnau (ed.), *Handbook of Complex Analysis: Geometric
  Function Theory I–II***. A large survey reference covering the
  modern research landscape.
* **Kenneth Stephenson, *Introduction to Circle Packing: The Theory of
  Discrete Analytic Functions*** (2005, [Cambridge University Press](https://www.cambridge.org/9780521823562)). Circle
  packings as discrete conformal maps, with Thurston's conjecture and the
  Rodin–Sullivan convergence theorem.
* **Gregory F. Lawler, *Conformally Invariant Processes in the
  Plane***. Where classical conformal invariance meets SLE and modern
  probability. Also listed in Section 8.
* **W. K. Hayman, *Multivalent Functions***. The classical monograph on
  multivalent functions and their extremal problems.

### More recent works

* **Donald E. Marshall, *Complex Analysis*** (2019, Cambridge University
  Press). A modern graduate text by a coauthor of *Harmonic Measure*,
  unusually good on conformal mapping and boundary behaviour.
* **Dmitry Beliaev, *Conformal Maps and Geometry*** (2019, [World
  Scientific](https://doi.org/10.1142/q0183)). Conformal mapping, harmonic
  measure, extremal length, Loewner evolution, and SLE in one graduate text.
* **Albert Baernstein II, *Symmetrization in Analysis*** (2019,
  [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781139020244)). Listed in full in Section 11. Symmetrization as
  an extremal method in geometric function theory.
* **Vladimir N. Dubinin, *Condenser Capacities and Symmetrization in
  Geometric Function Theory*** (2014,
  [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-0348-0843-9)). Capacities of
  condensers and symmetrization as methods for extremal problems.
* **Antti Kemppainen, *Schramm–Loewner Evolution*** (2017,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-65329-7), SpringerBriefs). A
  short introduction to SLE and its Loewner-chain background.
* **Nathanaël Berestycki and Ellen Powell, *Gaussian Free Field and
  Liouville Quantum Gravity*** (2025, [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781009405492)).
  Listed in full in Section 8. The random conformal geometry that continues
  the SLE direction of Lawler's book.

---

<a id="ckma-44"></a>

## 44. Riemann Surfaces, Quasiconformal Mappings, and Teichmüller Theory

Riemann surfaces, quasiconformal and quasiregular mappings, finite
distortion, and Teichmüller theory connect complex function theory with
surface geometry and deformation. Higher-dimensional quasiconformal
analysis, Kleinian groups, moduli, and surface mapping classes are included
where they support these analytic structures. Section 43 treats conformal
mapping and extremal methods, Section 39A metric quasiconformal viewpoints,
and Section 46 holomorphic dynamics and surgery. Spectral questions on
surfaces connect with Section 53.

### Foundational works

* **Hermann Weyl, *The Concept of a Riemann Surface***. Historically
  foundational and still conceptually illuminating.
* **Otto Forster, *Lectures on Riemann Surfaces***. The standard first
  graduate text, leading cleanly to sheaves, cohomology, and
  Riemann–Roch.
* **Hershel M. Farkas and Irwin Kra, *Riemann Surfaces***. The large
  classical treatise.
* **Rick Miranda, *Algebraic Curves and Riemann Surfaces***. The
  bridge between analytic and algebraic viewpoints.
* **Jürgen Jost, *Compact Riemann Surfaces***. A modern concise
  treatment emphasizing geometry and harmonic maps.
* **Raghavan Narasimhan, *Compact Riemann Surfaces***. Short and
  analytically oriented.
* **Robert C. Gunning, *Lectures on Riemann Surfaces***. Classical
  analytic and sheaf-theoretic methods.
* **Lars V. Ahlfors and Leo Sario, *Riemann Surfaces***. The
  mid-century reference for open surfaces and classification theory.
* **Lars V. Ahlfors, *Lectures on Quasiconformal Mappings***. The
  foundational analytic text, including the measurable Riemann mapping
  theorem.
* **Olli Lehto and K. I. Virtanen, *Quasiconformal Mappings in the
  Plane***. The classical comprehensive plane theory.
* **Kari Astala, Tadeusz Iwaniec, and Gaven J. Martin, *Elliptic Partial
  Differential Equations and Quasiconformal Mappings in the
  Plane***. Planar quasiconformal mappings developed through Sobolev spaces,
  singular integrals, and the Beltrami equation.
* **Tadeusz Iwaniec and Gaven J. Martin, *Geometric Function Theory and
  Non-linear Analysis***. Quasiregular mappings in higher dimensions
  and their PDE background.
* **Jussi Väisälä, *Lectures on n-Dimensional Quasiconformal
  Mappings***. The standard short treatment of the higher-dimensional
  theory.
* **Seppo Rickman, *Quasiregular Mappings***. A research monograph on
  quasiregular mappings and their higher-dimensional value-distribution
  theory.
* **Yoichi Imayoshi and Masahiko Taniguchi, *An Introduction to
  Teichmüller Spaces***. A compact standard introduction.
* **John H. Hubbard, *Teichmüller Theory and Applications to Geometry,
  Topology, and Dynamics***. A large modern geometric account.
* **Frederick P. Gardiner and Nikola Lakic, *Quasiconformal
  Teichmüller Theory***. The analytic theory in systematic form.
* **Athanase Papadopoulos (ed.), *Handbook of Teichmüller Theory***
  (several volumes, 2007–, European Mathematical Society; [I](https://ems.press/books/irma/35), [II](https://ems.press/books/irma/65), [III](https://ems.press/books/irma/113), [IV](https://ems.press/books/irma/231), [V](https://ems.press/books/irma/221), [VI](https://ems.press/books/irma/129), [VII](https://ems.press/books/irma/176)). A continuing
  survey handbook of Teichmüller theory and its neighbours.
* **Michael Kapovich, *Hyperbolic Manifolds and Discrete Groups*** (2001;
  reprinted 2009, [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-8176-4913-5)).
  Thurston's hyperbolization theorem for Haken manifolds, with the
  Kleinian-group and Teichmüller theory it needs.
* **Robert C. Gunning, *Riemann Surfaces and Generalized Theta Functions***. Theta functions
  on compact Riemann surfaces and the function theory built from them. Distinct from his
  *Lectures on Riemann Surfaces* and from Gunning and Rossi, *Analytic Functions of Several
  Complex Variables*.

### More recent works

* **Simon Donaldson, *Riemann Surfaces*** (2011, Oxford University
  Press). A modern geometric account with PDE methods prominent.
* **Wilhelm Schlag, *A Course in Complex Analysis and Riemann Surfaces***
  (2014, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/154)). Listed in full in Section 40.
* **Terrence Napier and Mohan Ramachandran, *An Introduction to Riemann
  Surfaces*** (2011, [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-8176-4693-6)).
  Riemann surfaces developed with analytic and geometric tools, including
  differential forms and potential theory. A substantial alternative to the
  standard short introductions.
* **Dror Varolin, *Riemann Surfaces by Way of Complex Analytic Geometry***
  (2011, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/125),
  Graduate Studies in Mathematics). Riemann surfaces through Hörmander's L²
  method, line bundles, and embeddings.
* **Stanislav Hencl and Pekka Koskela, *Lectures on Mappings of Finite
  Distortion*** (2014, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-03173-6), Lecture Notes in Mathematics).
  Mappings of finite distortion after the quasiconformal theory:
  differentiability, area, and the failure of the classical conclusions.
  Also listed in Section 5.
* **Frederick W. Gehring, Gaven J. Martin, and Bruce P. Palka, *An
  Introduction to the Theory of Higher-Dimensional Quasiconformal
  Mappings*** (2017, [American Mathematical
  Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/surv/216), Mathematical Surveys and
  Monographs). The analytic and geometric definitions, modulus of curve
  families, and the basic theory in R^n.
* **John H. Hubbard, *Teichmüller Theory and Applications to Geometry,
  Topology, and Dynamics*, volume 2: *Surface Homeomorphisms and Rational
  Functions*** (2016, [Matrix Editions](https://matrixeditions.com/TeichVol2.html)). Thurston's classification of surface
  homeomorphisms and his topological characterization of rational maps.
  Volume 1 is listed under Foundational works. Also listed in Section 46.
* **Scott A. Wolpert, *Families of Riemann Surfaces and Weil–Petersson
  Geometry*** (2010, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/cbms/113), CBMS). The
  Weil–Petersson metric and the geometry of moduli space, in a research
  course.
* **Benson Farb and Dan Margalit, *A Primer on Mapping Class
  Groups*** (2012, Princeton University Press). A topological introduction
  to mapping class groups, Teichmüller theory, and surface homeomorphisms,
  complementing the analytic references.
* **Albert Marden, *Hyperbolic Manifolds: An Introduction in 2 and 3
  Dimensions*** (2016, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9781316337776); revised edition of
  *Outer Circles*, 2007). Kleinian groups and
  hyperbolic 3-manifolds, with the quasiconformal methods of the
  Ahlfors–Bers school.
* **Nicolas Bergeron, *The Spectrum of Hyperbolic Surfaces*** (2016,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-27666-3); French original
  2011). Listed in full in Section 53. The recent geometric-spectral
  account; Peter Buser's *Geometry and Spectra of Compact Riemann
  Surfaces* (1992, reprinted 2010) is the classic.
* **Bodil Branner and Núria Fagella, *Quasiconformal Surgery in Holomorphic
  Dynamics*** (2014, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9781107337602)). Quasiconformal
  deformations used to construct holomorphic maps with prescribed dynamics. A
  research reference connecting Teichmüller methods with complex iteration.
* **Mario Bonk and Daniel Meyer, *Expanding Thurston Maps*** (2017,
  [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/surv/225), Mathematical Surveys and Monographs).
  Expanding Thurston maps, their visual metrics, and the relation with
  rational maps. Also listed in Section 46.

---

<a id="ckma-45"></a>

## 45. Several Complex Variables, Complex Manifolds, Pluripotential Theory, and CR Analysis

Several complex variables and complex geometry are organised into functions,
domains, and the d-bar equation (45A), complex manifolds, Stein spaces, and
Oka theory (45B), pluripotential theory and Kähler analysis (45C), and CR
geometry and analysis (45D). Local analytic, global geometric, and boundary
methods are distinguished while preserving their common holomorphic
framework. Section 42 treats analytic function spaces, Section 52 geometric
PDE and flows, and Section 53 Hodge, index, and Bergman-kernel methods on
manifolds.

<a id="ckma-45a"></a>

### 45A. Functions, Domains, and the d-bar Equation

Holomorphic functions, pseudoconvexity, integral representations, L^2
estimates, and boundary regularity provide the analytic theory of several
variables.

#### Foundational works

* **Lars Hörmander, *An Introduction to Complex Analysis in Several
  Variables***. A graduate treatment of pseudoconvexity, the d-bar equation,
  and L^2 estimates, leading to existence and extension theorems.

* **Steven G. Krantz, *Function Theory of Several Complex
  Variables***. The standard broad American graduate text, with
  extensive coverage of integral formulas and boundary behaviour.

* **Robert C. Gunning and Hugo Rossi, *Analytic Functions of Several
  Complex Variables***. The classical sheaf-theoretic foundation.

* **Klaus Fritzsche and Hans Grauert, *From Holomorphic Functions to
  Complex Manifolds***. A well-paced modern route from the local
  theory to complex manifolds.

* **Volker Scheidemann, *Introduction to Complex Analysis in Several
  Variables***. Short and clear; a good first exposure.

* **B. V. Shabat, *Introduction to Complex Analysis, Part II:
  Functions of Several Variables***. The classical Russian course.

* **Ludger Kaup and Burchard Kaup, *Holomorphic Functions of Several
  Variables***. A careful treatment with analytic sets and local
  algebra developed in parallel.

* **Raghavan Narasimhan, *Several Complex Variables***. Short, precise
  Chicago lectures.

* **Joseph L. Taylor, *Several Complex Variables with Connections to
  Algebraic Geometry and Lie Groups***. An unusual and valuable
  synthesis.

* **Herbert Alexander and John Wermer, *Several Complex Variables and
  Banach Algebras***. The uniform-algebra approach, with polynomial
  convexity treated thoroughly.

* **Marek Jarnicki and Peter Pflug, *First Steps in Several Complex
  Variables: Reinhardt Domains*** (2008, [European Mathematical
  Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/049)). Holomorphic convexity,
  pseudoconvexity, and invariant metrics worked out for Reinhardt domains.

* **R. Michael Range, *Holomorphic Functions and Integral
  Representations in Several Complex Variables***. Integral kernels,
  Bochner–Martinelli, Henkin–Ramirez, and boundary regularity.

* **Gennadi M. Henkin and Jürgen Leiterer, *Theory of Functions on
  Complex Manifolds*** and ***Andreotti–Grauert Theory by Integral
  Formulas***. The kernel-theoretic school at research level.

* **Christine Laurent-Thiébaut, *Holomorphic Function Theory in
  Several Variables***. A modern textbook treatment of integral
  representations and d-bar.

* **So-Chin Chen and Mei-Chi Shaw, *Partial Differential Equations in
  Several Complex Variables***. The PDE theory of d-bar and the
  d-bar-Neumann problem.

* **Gerald B. Folland and Joseph J. Kohn, *The Neumann Problem for the
  Cauchy–Riemann Complex***. The foundational monograph on
  subellipticity.

* **Takeo Ohsawa, *Analysis of Several Complex Variables***. Extension
  theorems and the L^2 method at research level; his later *L^2
  Approaches in Several Complex Variables* is listed below.

* **Ingo Lieb and Joachim Michel, *The Cauchy–Riemann Complex: Integral
  Formulae and Neumann Problem***. Integral representations and estimates
  for the d-bar-Neumann problem on strictly pseudoconvex domains.

* **Marek Jarnicki and Peter Pflug, *Invariant Distances and Metrics
  in Complex Analysis*** and ***Extension of Holomorphic
  Functions***. Kobayashi and Carathéodory metrics, and the systematic
  theory of extension.

* **Shoshichi Kobayashi, *Hyperbolic Complex Spaces***. A systematic treatment
  of invariant distances, holomorphic curves, and complex hyperbolicity.

* **Edgar Lee Stout, *Polynomial Convexity***. The standard reference on
  polynomially convex sets and polynomial approximation in several
  variables.

* **Walter Rudin, *Function Theory in the Unit Ball of C^n*** and
  ***Function Theory in Polydiscs***. Concrete model-domain function
  theory, invariant metrics, and boundary behaviour.

#### More recent works

* **Jiří Lebl, *Tasty Bits of Several Complex Variables***. A modern,
  freely available introduction; unusually good at motivating the
  subject.

* **Paul M. Gauthier, *Lectures on Several Complex Variables*** (2014,
  [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-11511-5)). A compact graduate
  course in holomorphic functions, domains, and approximation in several
  variables. Useful as an entry before the larger SCV monographs.

* **Steven G. Krantz, *Harmonic and Complex Analysis in Several Variables***
  (2017, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-63231-5)). Real harmonic
  analysis and several-variable holomorphic function theory developed in
  parallel. Makes the shared kernels, boundary phenomena, and analytic methods
  visible.

* **Emil J. Straube, *Lectures on the L^2-Sobolev Theory of the
  d-bar-Neumann Problem*** (2010, European Mathematical Society). L^2 and
  Sobolev estimates for the d-bar-Neumann problem, with emphasis on
  regularity, compactness, and their geometric conditions.

* **Takeo Ohsawa, *L² Approaches in Several Complex Variables: Towards the
  Oka–Cartan Theory with Precise Bounds*** (2015; 2nd ed., 2018,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-4-431-56852-0)). The d-bar
  equation, L^2 extension theorems, and Oka–Cartan theory, with quantitative
  estimates. A research-level continuation of *Analysis of Several Complex
  Variables*.

* **Friedrich Haslinger, *The ∂̄-Neumann Problem and Schrödinger
  Operators*** (2nd ed., 2023, [De Gruyter](https://doi.org/10.1515/9783111182926)). The ∂̄-Neumann problem
  through Schrödinger operators with magnetic fields. The second
  edition updates the magnetic theory and the compactness criteria.

<a id="ckma-45b"></a>

### 45B. Complex Manifolds, Stein Spaces, and Oka Theory

Sheaves, analytic sets, holomorphic bundles, Stein geometry, and holomorphic
approximation connect local complex analysis with global geometric and
homotopy problems.

#### Foundational works

* **R. O. Wells, Jr., *Differential Analysis on Complex
  Manifolds***. Dolbeault cohomology, Hodge theory, and compact
  complex manifolds.

* **Daniel Huybrechts, *Complex Geometry: An Introduction***. The
  standard modern bridge between the analytic and algebraic sides.

* **Phillip Griffiths and Joseph Harris, *Principles of Algebraic
  Geometry***. Its first chapters remain a standard analytic reference
  for Hodge theory and residues.

* **Hans Grauert and Reinhold Remmert, *Theory of Stein Spaces*** and
  ***Coherent Analytic Sheaves***. The classical structural
  references.

* **E. M. Chirka, *Complex Analytic Sets***. Local and global structure of
  analytic sets, multiplicity, and extension theorems.

#### More recent works

* **Tatsuo Suwa, *Complex Analytic Geometry: From the Localization
  Viewpoint*** (2024, [World Scientific](https://doi.org/10.1142/8324)).
  Local analytic geometry, residues, and index theorems.

* **Junjiro Noguchi, *Analytic Function Theory of Several Variables:
  Elements of Oka's Coherence*** (2016,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-981-10-0291-5)). Oka's coherence
  theorems and the Cousin problems, following Oka's own approach.

* **John M. Lee, *Introduction to Complex Manifolds*** (2024, [American
  Mathematical Society](https://sites.math.washington.edu/~lee/Books/ICM/)).
  Complex and Kähler geometry, holomorphic bundles, cohomology, and analytic
  tools leading to the Kodaira embedding theorem. A graduate course building
  on smooth manifolds and Riemannian geometry.

* **Franc Forstnerič, *Stein Manifolds and Holomorphic
  Mappings*** (2011; 2nd ed., 2017, Springer). Stein manifolds, holomorphic
  approximation, and the Oka principle, including homotopy methods for
  holomorphic mappings.

* **Antonio Alarcón, Franc Forstnerič, and Francisco J. López, *Minimal
  Surfaces from a Complex Analytic Viewpoint*** (2021, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-69056-4)).
  Minimal surfaces through holomorphic curves, the Oka principle, and
  Riemann–Hilbert problems.
  Also listed in Section 52.

<a id="ckma-45c"></a>

### 45C. Pluripotential Theory and Kähler Analysis

Plurisubharmonic functions, positive currents, complex Monge–Ampère
equations, and canonical metrics connect nonlinear potential theory with
Kähler geometry.

#### Foundational works

* **Jean-Pierre Demailly, *Complex Analytic and Differential
  Geometry***. Freely available lecture notes on complex manifolds,
  currents, positivity, vanishing theorems, and L^2 methods.

* **Jean-Pierre Demailly, *Analytic Methods in Algebraic
  Geometry***. Positive currents, multiplier ideals, and L^2 extension
  applied to algebraic geometry.

* **Pierre Lelong and Lawrence Gruman, *Entire Functions of Several
  Complex Variables***. Growth, currents, and value distribution in
  several variables.

* **Maciej Klimek, *Pluripotential Theory***. The standard monograph
  on plurisubharmonic functions and the complex Monge–Ampère operator.

* **Lars Hörmander, *Notions of Convexity***. Plurisubharmonic functions,
  the minimum principle, and the convexity theorems that organise
  several complex variables. Also listed in Section 41, where the same
  book is used for entire functions.

* **Sławomir Kołodziej, *The Complex Monge–Ampère Equation and
  Pluripotential Theory*** (2005, [American Mathematical
  Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/memo/0840), Memoirs). Weak solutions with
  L^p densities and capacity estimates; a foundation for Guedj–Zeriahi.

* **Gang Tian, *Canonical Metrics in Kähler Geometry*** (2000,
  [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-0348-8389-4)). Kähler–Einstein
  metrics, the Calabi–Yau theorem, and K-stability as it stood in 2000.

#### More recent works

* **Vincent Guedj and Ahmed Zeriahi, *Degenerate Complex Monge–Ampère
  Equations*** (2017, European Mathematical Society). A research treatment
  of degenerate complex Monge–Ampère equations through pluripotential
  theory, energy classes, and applications to Kähler geometry.

* **Vincent Guedj (ed.), *Complex Monge–Ampère Equations and Geodesics in the
  Space of Kähler Metrics*** (2012,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-23669-3)). Graduate lecture
  courses on Monge–Ampère operators, boundary problems, the Calabi conjecture,
  and geodesics in spaces of metrics. A useful bridge between pluripotential
  theory and Kähler geometry.

* **Sébastien Boucksom, Philippe Eyssidieux, and Vincent Guedj (eds.),
  *An Introduction to the Kähler–Ricci Flow*** (2013, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-00819-6), Lecture
  Notes in Mathematics). Lecture notes making the Kähler–Ricci flow
  accessible from complex analysis.

* **Gábor Székelyhidi, *An Introduction to Extremal Kähler Metrics***
  (2014, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/152), Graduate Studies in
  Mathematics). A graduate introduction to cscK metrics, K-stability,
  and the analytic problems around them.
  Also listed in Section 52.

* **Junjiro Noguchi and Jörg Winkelmann, *Nevanlinna Theory in Several
  Complex Variables and Diophantine Approximation*** (2014, Springer).
  Value distribution in the several-variable setting.

<a id="ckma-45d"></a>

### 45D. CR Geometry and Analysis

Real submanifolds, tangential Cauchy–Riemann equations, finite type, and
mapping problems describe the analytic geometry of complex boundaries.

#### Foundational works

* **Albert Boggess, *CR Manifolds and the Tangential Cauchy–Riemann
  Complex***. The standard introduction.

* **M. Salah Baouendi, Peter Ebenfelt, and Linda Preiss Rothschild,
  *Real Submanifolds in Complex Space and Their Mappings***. A
  research monograph on finite type, reflection, and mapping problems.

* **John P. D'Angelo, *Several Complex Variables and the Geometry of
  Real Hypersurfaces***. Finite type and local hypersurface geometry.

* **Sorin Dragomir and Giuseppe Tomassini, *Differential Geometry and
  Analysis on CR Manifolds***. The geometric side, with subelliptic
  analysis included.

* **Shiferaw Berhanu, Paulo D. Cordaro, and Jorge Hounie, *An
  Introduction to Involutive Structures*** (2008, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511543067)). Involutive structures and locally integrable systems, the
  wider setting of CR geometry.

#### More recent works

* **John P. D'Angelo, *Hermitian Analysis: From Fourier Series to
  Cauchy–Riemann Geometry*** (2nd ed., 2019, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-16514-7)). CR geometry
  approached from Fourier series and Hermitian forms. Distinct from his
  hypersurface monograph.

* **John P. D'Angelo, *Rational Sphere Maps*** (2021,
  [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-75809-7)). Rational maps
  between complex spheres, proper holomorphic maps, and CR geometry. A
  specialised synthesis relating Hermitian forms, degree bounds, and geometric
  mapping problems.


---

<a id="ckma-46"></a>

## 46. Complex Dynamics

Iteration of holomorphic maps is studied through Fatou and Julia sets,
stability, renormalization, invariant measures, and the geometry of limit
sets. Rational, transcendental, several-variable, and non-Archimedean
dynamics are represented, together with arithmetic and thermodynamic
connections. Section 31 supplies general dynamical methods, Section 10
ergodic theory, and Section 44 quasiconformal deformation and surface
geometry. Value distribution and fractal dimensions connect with Sections 41
and 39A.

### Foundational works

* **John Milnor, *Dynamics in One Complex Variable***. The standard
  modern introduction to the iteration of rational maps.
* **Lennart Carleson and Theodore W. Gamelin, *Complex Dynamics***. A
  concise analytic graduate text.
* **Alan F. Beardon, *Iteration of Rational Functions***. A broad
  classical treatment of Julia and Fatou theory.
* **Norbert Steinmetz, *Rational Iteration***. A compact
  research-level account.
* **Curtis T. McMullen, *Complex Dynamics and Renormalization*** and
  ***Renormalization and 3-Manifolds Which Fiber over the
  Circle***. Research monographs on rigidity and renormalization.
* **R. Daniel Mauldin and Mariusz Urbański, *Graph Directed Markov
  Systems: Geometry and Dynamics of Limit Sets*** (2003, [Cambridge
  University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511543050)). Limit sets
  of graph-directed systems, their dimensions and measures, and the
  thermodynamic formalism used to study them.
* **Joseph H. Silverman, *The Arithmetic of Dynamical Systems*** (2007,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-387-69904-2), Graduate Texts in
  Mathematics). Rational maps over number fields and p-adic fields; the
  arithmetic counterpart of one-variable dynamics.

### More recent works

* **Feliks Przytycki and Mariusz Urbański, *Conformal Fractals:
  Ergodic Theory Methods*** (2010, Cambridge University Press).
  Thermodynamic formalism in conformal dynamics and fractal geometry.
* **Mariusz Urbański, Mario Roy, and Sara Munday, *Non-Invertible
  Dynamical Systems*** (multi-volume, 2022–, De Gruyter). The modern
  systematic treatment of thermodynamic formalism for conformal systems.
* **Janina Kotus and Mariusz Urbański, *Meromorphic Dynamics*** (2 volumes,
  2023, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781009215930); [volume II](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781009215985)). The ergodic theory and
  fractal geometry of transcendental meromorphic functions.
* **Mario Bonk and Daniel Meyer, *Expanding Thurston Maps*** (2017,
  [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/surv/225)). Listed in full in Section 44.
* **John H. Hubbard, *Teichmüller Theory*, volume 2** (2016, [Matrix
  Editions](https://matrixeditions.com/TeichVol2.html)). Listed in full in Section 44. Thurston's characterization of rational
  maps.
* **Marco Abate, Eric Bedford, Marco Brunella, Tien-Cuong Dinh, Dierk
  Schleicher, and Nessim Sibony, *Holomorphic Dynamical Systems*** (2010,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-13171-4), Lecture Notes in
  Mathematics). CIME lectures on dynamics in several complex variables,
  holomorphic foliations, and transcendental dynamics.
* **Marco Abate, *Holomorphic Dynamics on Hyperbolic Riemann
  Surfaces*** (De Gruyter) and the several-variable literature of Bedford,
  Fornaess, and Sibony. Holomorphic self-maps of hyperbolic Riemann
  surfaces, their iteration, and the invariant metrics used in their study.
* **Matthew Baker and Robert Rumely, *Potential Theory and Dynamics on the
  Berkovich Projective Line*** (2010, [American Mathematical
  Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/surv/159), Mathematical Surveys and
  Monographs). Laplacians, harmonic measure, and equilibrium measures on the
  Berkovich line: non-archimedean potential theory.
* **Robert L. Benedetto, *Dynamics in One Non-Archimedean Variable*** (2019,
  [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/198), Graduate
  Studies in Mathematics). Fatou and Julia sets over non-archimedean fields,
  as a graduate course.
* **Daniel S. Alexander, Felice Iavernaro, and Alessandro Rosa, *Early
  Days of Complex Dynamics*** (2012, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/hmath/038)).
  Listed in full in Section 2.

---

# Part VIII. Classical Analysis: Approximation, Special Functions, Asymptotics

---

<a id="ckma-47"></a>

## 47. Approximation Theory

Approximation by polynomials, rational functions, splines, kernels, ridge
functions, and sparse expansions is studied through error estimates, moduli
of smoothness, widths, and direct and inverse theorems. Multivariate,
spherical, and high-dimensional settings extend the classical theory.
Section 26 supplies smoothness and interpolation spaces, Section 29
structured expansions and sampling, and Section 48 orthogonal polynomials
and weighted potential methods. Computational references are included where
their mathematical approximation theory is central.

### Foundational works

* **Ronald A. DeVore and George G. Lorentz, *Constructive
  Approximation***. The principal modern reference for direct and
  inverse theorems, moduli of smoothness, and nonlinear approximation.
* **George G. Lorentz, Manfred von Golitschek, and Yuly Makovoz,
  *Constructive Approximation: Advanced Problems***. Advanced material
  extending the DeVore–Lorentz programme.
* **E. W. Cheney, *Introduction to Approximation Theory***. A classic
  graduate introduction to best approximation and Chebyshev systems.
* **T. J. Rivlin, *An Introduction to the Approximation of
  Functions*** and ***Chebyshev Polynomials***. An introduction to uniform
  approximation, together with a focused treatment of Chebyshev polynomials
  and their extremal properties.
* **N. I. Achieser, *Theory of Approximation***. A classical large
  reference on polynomial and rational approximation.
* **Andrei Bogatyrev, *Extremal Polynomials and Riemann Surfaces*** (2012;
  English translation of the 2005 Russian book,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-25634-9)). Polynomial extremal
  problems solved through Riemann surfaces and complex function theory. A
  research monograph linking Chebyshev-type approximation with geometry.
* **A. F. Timan, *Theory of Approximation of Functions of a Real
  Variable***. The standard classical source for Jackson- and
  Bernstein-type theorems.
* **George G. Lorentz, *Approximation of Functions*** and ***Bernstein
  Polynomials***. Classical constructive approximation, together with a
  specialised treatment of Bernstein polynomials and their approximation
  properties.
* **Zeev Ditzian and Vilmos Totik, *Moduli of Smoothness***. The
  reference for weighted and nonuniform moduli and the associated
  direct and converse theorems.
* **Qazi I. Rahman and Gerhard Schmeisser, *Analytic Theory of Polynomials***
  (2002, [Oxford University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1093/oso/9780198534938.001.0001)). Zeros and
  critical points of polynomials, and the Bernstein and Markov inequalities,
  in one reference.
* **Allan Pinkus, *n-Widths in Approximation Theory***. The standard
  research monograph on widths and optimal recovery.
* **Erich Novak and Henryk Woźniakowski, *Tractability of Multivariate
  Problems*** (3 volumes, 2008–2012, European Mathematical Society; [I](https://ems.press/books/etm/56), [II](https://ems.press/books/etm/83), [III](https://ems.press/books/etm/116)).
  Information-based complexity and the tractability of high-dimensional
  approximation.
* **Allan Pinkus, *Totally Positive Matrices***. Variation-diminishing
  transformations, splines, and total positivity.
* **Samuel Karlin, *Total Positivity***. The large classical reference
  on the same subject.
* **Larry L. Schumaker, *Spline Functions: Basic Theory***. The
  standard mathematical reference for splines.
* **Carl de Boor, *A Practical Guide to Splines***. Spline theory and
  computation developed together, including B-splines, interpolation, and
  approximation.
* **Holger Wendland, *Scattered Data Approximation*** (2005, [Cambridge
  University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511617539)). Radial basis
  functions, native spaces, and error estimates for scattered-data
  interpolation.
* **Herbert Stahl and Vilmos Totik, *General Orthogonal
  Polynomials***. Approximation and potential theory meeting
  orthogonal polynomials.
* **Edward B. Saff and Vilmos Totik, *Logarithmic Potentials with
  External Fields***. Weighted approximation and equilibrium problems.
  Also listed in Section 38.
* **Dietrich Braess, *Nonlinear Approximation Theory***. Rational and
  nonlinear approximation in systematic form.
* **George A. Baker Jr. and Peter Graves-Morris, *Padé
  Approximants***. The standard reference on Padé approximation and its
  convergence theory.
* **Peter Borwein and Tamás Erdélyi, *Polynomials and Polynomial
  Inequalities***. Markov, Bernstein, and Remez inequalities and Müntz
  theorems.
* **Ming-Jun Lai and Larry L. Schumaker, *Spline Functions on
  Triangulations***. The standard reference on multivariate splines.
* **Giuseppe Mastroianni and Gradimir V. Milovanović, *Interpolation
  Processes: Basic Theory and Applications***. Lagrange interpolation,
  quadrature, and weighted polynomial approximation.
* **Walter Edwin Sewell, *Degree of Approximation by Polynomials in the
  Complex Domain***. The classical monograph on Jackson-type estimates in
  the complex domain.
* **Lisa Lorentzen and Haakon Waadeland, *Continued Fractions, Volume 1:
  Convergence Theory***. The convergence theory of continued fractions,
  with value regions and truncation-error bounds.
* **Martin D. Buhmann, *Radial Basis Functions: Theory and
  Implementations***. Approximation by radial basis functions.
* **Carl de Boor, Klaus Höllig, and Sherman Riemenschneider, *Box Splines***. Structure,
  approximation order, and Fourier analysis of box splines. Distinct from de Boor's *A
  Practical Guide to Splines*.
* **Michael Drmota and Robert F. Tichy, *Sequences, Discrepancies and Applications***.
  Uniform distribution and discrepancy, with applications in analysis. Dick and
  Pillichshammer treat the quasi-Monte Carlo side below.

### More recent works

#### Constructive and polynomial approximation

* **Lloyd N. Trefethen, *Approximation Theory and Approximation
  Practice*** (2013; 2nd ed., 2019, SIAM). Computational in emphasis,
  but an exceptionally effective modern account of polynomial and
  rational approximation and of Chebfun-style thinking.
* **Jorge Bustamante, *Algebraic Approximation: A Guide to Past and Current
  Solutions*** (2012,
  [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-0348-0194-2)). Polynomial
  approximation, direct and inverse estimates, and smoothness. A
  research-oriented continuation of the classical constructive-approximation
  books.
* **Larry L. Schumaker, *Spline Functions: Computational Methods***
  (2015, [SIAM](https://doi.org/10.1137/1.9781611973907)). The computational companion to Schumaker's *Basic
  Theory*. Included for the approximation theory, not as a numerical
  manual.
* **Vilmos Totik, *Polynomial Approximation on Polytopes*** (2014, [American Mathematical
  Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/memo/1091), Memoirs of the American Mathematical
  Society). Polynomial approximation on polytopes, with inverse theorems and the role of the
  boundary.

#### Approximation on spheres and balls

* **Kendall Atkinson and Weimin Han, *Spherical Harmonics and Approximations
  on the Unit Sphere: An Introduction*** (2012,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-25983-8)). Spherical harmonics,
  approximation, and integral operators on the sphere. A focused graduate
  route into approximation on curved domains.
* **Feng Dai and Yuan Xu, *Approximation Theory and Harmonic Analysis on
  Spheres and Balls*** (2013,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4614-6660-4)). Orthogonal
  expansions, moduli of smoothness, and approximation inequalities on spheres
  and balls. A substantial specialist reference complementing Euclidean
  approximation theory.
* **Volker Michel, *Lectures on Constructive Approximation: Fourier, Spline,
  and Wavelet Methods on the Real Line, the Sphere, and the Ball*** (2013,
  [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-8176-8403-7)). A graduate course
  comparing several approximation systems on Euclidean and spherical domains.
  Useful for relating abstract estimates to concrete expansions.

#### High-dimensional, sparse, and nonlinear approximation

* **Vladimir Temlyakov, *Greedy Approximation*** (2011, Cambridge
  University Press) and ***Multivariate Approximation*** (2018, [Cambridge
  University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781108689687)). Nonlinear and
  high-dimensional approximation at research level: greedy algorithms,
  approximation of function classes in several variables, hyperbolic-cross
  methods, and discretisation.
* **Vladimir Temlyakov, *Sparse Approximation with Bases*** (2015,
  [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-0348-0890-3)). Sparse expansions,
  greedy methods, and approximation with bases. A focused continuation of the
  broader *Greedy Approximation*.
* **Dinh Dũng, Vladimir Temlyakov, and Tino Ullrich, *Hyperbolic Cross
  Approximation*** (2018, [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-92240-9)). Hyperbolic crosses, mixed
  smoothness, and high-dimensional approximation, at research level.
* **Ben Adcock, Simone Brugiapaglia, and Clayton G. Webster, *Sparse
  Polynomial Approximation of High-Dimensional Functions*** (2022,
  [SIAM](https://doi.org/10.1137/1.9781611976885)). Sparse polynomial approximation and the curse of
  dimensionality.
* **Josef Dick and Friedrich Pillichshammer, *Digital Nets and
  Sequences: Discrepancy Theory and Quasi-Monte Carlo Integration***
  (2010, [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511761188)). Discrepancy and quasi-Monte Carlo,
  on the analytic side of high-dimensional integration.
* **Allan Pinkus, *Ridge Functions*** (2015, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9781316408124)). Density,
  representation, and approximation by ridge functions; the mathematics
  behind one-layer neural networks.

---

<a id="ckma-48"></a>

## 48. Orthogonal Polynomials, Moment Problems, and Spectral Recurrences

Orthogonal polynomials on the line, circle, and higher-dimensional domains
connect recurrence relations with measures, approximation, and spectral
theory. Classical and multidimensional moment problems, positivity,
continued fractions, varying weights, and Christoffel functions are
included. Section 22 treats the associated operators, Section 47
approximation and logarithmic potentials, and Section 49 special-function
families. Random matrix asymptotics and nonlinear recurrence equations
connect this section with Sections 7, 50, and 51.

### Foundational works

* **Gábor Szegő, *Orthogonal Polynomials***. The classical
  foundational monograph; still the first reference for asymptotics
  and the classical families.
* **Theodore S. Chihara, *An Introduction to Orthogonal
  Polynomials***. A standard systematic graduate account built on the
  moment-problem viewpoint.
* **Mourad E. H. Ismail, *Classical and Quantum Orthogonal Polynomials
  in One Variable***. The modern research reference, including the
  q-theory and the Askey scheme.
* **Géza Freud, *Orthogonal Polynomials***. Classical weighted and
  asymptotic theory.
* **Eli Levin and Doron S. Lubinsky, *Orthogonal Polynomials for Exponential
  Weights*** (2001, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4613-0201-8),
  CMS Books in Mathematics). The theory of Freud and exponential weights in
  full.
* **Walter Gautschi, *Orthogonal Polynomials: Computation and
  Approximation*** (2004, [Oxford University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1093/oso/9780198506720.001.0001)). The
  constructive theory: modified moments, Gauss quadrature, and the
  computation of recurrence coefficients.
* **Barry Simon, *Orthogonal Polynomials on the Unit Circle
  I–II***. A two-volume treatment of orthogonal polynomials on the unit
  circle, connecting recurrence relations and measure theory with spectral
  theory.
* **Percy Deift, *Orthogonal Polynomials and Random Matrices: A
  Riemann–Hilbert Approach***. The entry point to steepest-descent
  asymptotics.
  Also listed in Sections 7 and 51.
* **Charles F. Dunkl and Yuan Xu, *Orthogonal Polynomials of Several
  Variables***. The standard reference for the multivariate theory.
* **Gerald Teschl, *Jacobi Operators and Completely Integrable Nonlinear
  Lattices*** (2000, [American Mathematical
  Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/surv/072), Mathematical Surveys and
  Monographs). The spectral theory of Jacobi operators and the Toda lattice.
* **N. I. Akhiezer, *The Classical Moment Problem and Some Related
  Questions in Analysis***. The foundational analytic account of the
  Hamburger and Stieltjes problems.
* **J. A. Shohat and J. D. Tamarkin, *The Problem of Moments***. The
  classical large reference.
* **Mark G. Krein and A. A. Nudel'man, *The Markov Moment Problem and
  Extremal Problems***. The extremal-problem viewpoint, with many
  applications.
* **Murray Marshall, *Positive Polynomials and Sums of Squares*** (2008,
  [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/surv/146),
  Mathematical Surveys and Monographs). Positivstellensätze and their
  duality with moment problems.
* **Vilmos Totik, *Weighted Approximation with Varying
  Weight***. Where orthogonal polynomials, potential theory, and
  approximation meet.
* **Sergey Khrushchev, *Orthogonal Polynomials and Continued Fractions:
  From Euler's Point of View***. Orthogonal polynomials on the unit circle
  through continued fractions and Schur functions.
* **Adhemar Bultheel, Pablo González-Vera, Erik Hendriksen, and Olav
  Njåstad, *Orthogonal Rational Functions***. Rational analogues of
  orthogonal polynomials and Szegő theory.
* **A. F. Nikiforov, S. K. Suslov, and V. B. Uvarov, *Classical
  Orthogonal Polynomials of a Discrete Variable***. Hahn, Meixner,
  Krawtchouk, and Racah polynomials.

### More recent works

* **Roelof Koekoek, Peter A. Lesky, and René F. Swarttouw,
  *Hypergeometric Orthogonal Polynomials and Their q-Analogues***
  (2010, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-05014-5)). The reference catalogue of the Askey scheme, with
  the formulas and the limits between families.
  Also listed in Sections 49 and 54.
* **Howard S. Cohl and Mourad E. H. Ismail (eds.), *Lectures on Orthogonal
  Polynomials and Special Functions*** (2021, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781108908993)). A collection of substantial
  graduate lecture courses on orthogonal polynomials and related special
  functions. Useful for entering selected modern directions rather than as a
  replacement for Szegő.
* **Milivoje Lukić, *A First Course in Spectral Theory*** (2022,
  [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/226)). Listed in full in Section 22. Jacobi
  operators and the spectral form of orthogonal polynomials.
* **Barry Simon, *Szegő's Theorem and Its Descendants*** (2011,
  Princeton University Press). A synthesis of sum rules, spectral
  theory, and the Szegő asymptotics across settings.
* **Konrad Schmüdgen, *The Moment Problem*** (2017, Springer). The
  modern comprehensive treatment of one- and multi-dimensional moment
  problems, positivity, and representing measures.
* **Jean B. Lasserre, *Moments, Positive Polynomials and Their
  Applications*** (2010, [Imperial College Press](https://doi.org/10.1142/p665)). The moment problem
  from the side of positive polynomials and optimisation, complementary
  to Schmüdgen.
* **Jean B. Lasserre, Edouard Pauwels, and Mihai Putinar, *The
  Christoffel–Darboux Kernel for Data Analysis*** (2022, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781108937078)). Christoffel functions,
  moments, and polynomial kernels used to recover geometric information from
  measures. Included for the analytic and moment-theoretic foundations.
* **Eli Levin and Doron S. Lubinsky, *Bounds and Asymptotics for Orthogonal
  Polynomials for Varying Weights*** (2018,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-72947-3)). Estimates and
  limiting behaviour when the weight varies with the degree. A research
  account useful in weighted approximation and random-matrix asymptotics.
* **David Damanik and Jake Fillman, *One-Dimensional Ergodic
  Schrödinger Operators*** (2022–2025, American Mathematical Society; [I](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/221), [II](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/249)).
  Listed in full in Section 22. The ergodic theory of the recurrences.
* **Walter Van Assche, *Orthogonal Polynomials and Painlevé
  Equations*** (2017, Cambridge University Press). Recurrence
  coefficients, integrable systems, and special functions.
* **Nalini Joshi, *Discrete Painlevé Equations*** (2019,
  [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/cbms/131)). Listed in full in Section 51.
  The discrete Painlevé companion to Van Assche's book.

---

<a id="ckma-49"></a>

## 49. Special Functions, Integral Transforms, and Classical Formulae

Special functions, hypergeometric series, integral transforms, fractional
calculus, and complete monotonicity form a repertoire of explicit analytic
constructions and identities. Systematic texts sit alongside handbooks,
tables, and computational treatments, with attention to representations,
transformations, and the equations defining the functions. Section 48
develops orthogonal polynomials, Section 50 asymptotic methods, and Section
51 integrable and monodromy problems. Section 54 collects reference tools by
their practical role rather than by mathematical subject.

### Foundational works

#### Graduate texts

* **George E. Andrews, Richard Askey, and Ranjan Roy, *Special
  Functions***. A graduate treatment of gamma and beta functions,
  hypergeometric and basic hypergeometric series, orthogonal polynomials,
  and partitions.
* **E. T. Whittaker and G. N. Watson, *A Course of Modern
  Analysis***. The classical source for gamma, elliptic,
  hypergeometric, and contour-integral methods, and a model of how
  analysis was written.
* **N. N. Lebedev, *Special Functions and Their Applications***. A
  concise classical graduate account.
* **Nico M. Temme, *Special Functions: An Introduction to the
  Classical Functions of Mathematical Physics***. A modern analytic
  treatment with asymptotics throughout. His *Asymptotic Methods for
  Integrals* (2015) is listed in Section 50.
* **Earl D. Rainville, *Special Functions***. A classical account of
  the special functions through generating functions and
  hypergeometric series.
* **B. C. Carlson, *Special Functions of Applied Mathematics***. The
  Dirichlet-average approach to special functions, relating classical
  families through integration over simplices.
* **A. F. Nikiforov and V. B. Uvarov, *Special Functions of Mathematical
  Physics***. Special functions developed from the hypergeometric-type
  differential equation.
* **Claus Müller, *Spherical Harmonics*** and ***Analysis of Spherical
  Symmetries in Euclidean Spaces***. Spherical harmonics in arbitrary
  dimensions, from Müller's classic lecture notes to the later full
  treatment.
* **Willard Miller Jr., *Lie Theory and Special Functions***. Special
  functions as matrix elements of Lie group representations.
* **N. Ja. Vilenkin and A. U. Klimyk, *Representation of Lie Groups and
  Special Functions I–III***. The encyclopedic treatment of special
  functions as matrix elements of representations.
* **André Weil, *Elliptic Functions according to Eisenstein and
  Kronecker***. Eisenstein series and the Kronecker limit formula.

#### Handbooks, tables, and computation

* **Arthur Erdélyi, Wilhelm Magnus, Fritz Oberhettinger, and Francesco
  G. Tricomi, *Higher Transcendental Functions*** (3 volumes, the
  Bateman Manuscript Project). An extensive reference for classical special
  functions, including transformation, connection, and integral formulas.
* **Arthur Erdélyi et al., *Tables of Integral Transforms*** (2
  volumes). The companion transform reference.
* **Wilhelm Magnus, Fritz Oberhettinger, and Raj Pal Soni, *Formulas
  and Theorems for the Special Functions of Mathematical Physics***. A
  compact and reliable formula handbook.
* **I. S. Gradshteyn and I. M. Ryzhik, *Table of Integrals, Series,
  and Products***. A large reference collection of definite and indefinite
  integrals, series, and products.
  Also listed in Section 54.
* **A. P. Prudnikov, Yu. A. Brychkov, and O. I. Marichev, *Integrals
  and Series*** (5 volumes). Extensive tables of integrals, series, and
  integral transforms, including formulas involving special functions.
  Also listed in Section 54.
* **Yudell L. Luke, *The Special Functions and Their Approximations***
  (2 volumes). Computation-oriented, and rich in rational and
  Chebyshev approximations.
* **Annie Cuyt, Vigdis Brevik Petersen, Brigitte Verdonk, Haakon
  Waadeland, and William B. Jones, *Handbook of Continued Fractions for
  Special Functions*** (2008, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4020-6949-9)). Continued-fraction
  representations of the classical special functions.
* **Amparo Gil, Javier Segura, and Nico M. Temme, *Numerical Methods for
  Special Functions*** (2007,
  [SIAM](https://doi.org/10.1137/1.9780898717822)). Recurrences, quadrature,
  continued fractions, and uniform asymptotics as computational tools.
* **Fritz Oberhettinger, *Tables of Bessel Transforms***, ***Tables of
  Mellin Transforms***, and ***Tables of Fourier Transforms and Fourier
  Transforms of Distributions***; and **Oberhettinger and Larry Badii,
  *Tables of Laplace Transforms***. Standard reference tables of integral
  transforms.

#### Hypergeometric series and Bessel functions

* **G. N. Watson, *A Treatise on the Theory of Bessel
  Functions***. A classical research treatise on Bessel functions, including
  representations, asymptotics, zeros, and related expansions.
* **Lucy Joan Slater, *Generalized Hypergeometric Functions***. The
  classical monograph on the generalized hypergeometric series.
* **W. N. Bailey, *Generalized Hypergeometric Series***. The
  classical tract on generalized hypergeometric series.
* **George Gasper and Mizan Rahman, *Basic Hypergeometric Series***.
  The modern standard reference for basic hypergeometric series.
* **Kazuhiko Aomoto and Michitake Kita, *Theory of Hypergeometric
  Functions*** (2011, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-4-431-53938-4);
  Japanese original 1994). Hypergeometric integrals in several variables
  through twisted de Rham theory.
* **George E. Andrews and Bruce C. Berndt, *Ramanujan's Lost Notebook*** (5
  parts, 2005–2018, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/0-387-28124-X)). Mock
  theta functions, q-series, and integrals, with proofs. The link is to Part
  I.
* **Katsunori Iwasaki, Hironobu Kimura, Shun Shimomura, and Masaaki
  Yoshida, *From Gauss to Painlevé: A Modern Theory of Special
  Functions***. Hypergeometric equations, monodromy, and the Painlevé
  equations.

#### Integral transforms, fractional calculus, and complete monotonicity

* **David V. Widder, *The Laplace Transform*** and ***An Introduction
  to Transform Theory***. The classical rigorous monographs on Laplace
  inversion and completely monotone functions.
* **E. C. Titchmarsh, *Introduction to the Theory of Fourier
  Integrals***. The classical Fourier transform reference on the line.
  Also listed in Section 27.
* **Ian N. Sneddon, *The Use of Integral Transforms***. Fourier,
  Laplace, Hankel, and Mellin transforms in applications.
* **Ricardo Estrada and Ram P. Kanwal, *A Distributional Approach to
  Asymptotics***. Transforms and asymptotics in the distributional
  setting.
* **Anatoly A. Kilbas, Hari M. Srivastava, and Juan J. Trujillo, *Theory and
  Applications of Fractional Differential Equations*** (2006,
  [Elsevier](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0304-0208(06)x8001-5), North-Holland
  Mathematics Studies). The standard reference for fractional integrals and
  derivatives and the equations built from them.
* **René L. Schilling, Renming Song, and Zoran Vondraček, *Bernstein
  Functions***. Complete monotonicity and its many appearances across
  analysis and probability.
* **I. I. Hirschman and D. V. Widder, *The Convolution Transform***.
  Inversion of convolution transforms by differential operators.

### More recent works

* **NIST Digital Library of Mathematical Functions**, and **Frank
  W. J. Olver, Daniel W. Lozier, Ronald F. Boisvert, and Charles
  W. Clark (eds.), *NIST Handbook of Mathematical Functions*** (2010,
  Cambridge University Press). A reference for special-function definitions,
  identities, differential equations, and asymptotics, available as an
  online library and a print handbook.
* **Richard Beals and Roderick Wong, *Special Functions and Orthogonal
  Polynomials*** (2016, Cambridge University Press). A modern rigorous
  graduate text.
* **Carlo Viola, *An Introduction to Special Functions*** (2016,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-41345-7)). A graduate course on
  gamma, hypergeometric, Bessel, and related functions. Useful for learning
  the analytic theory behind the reference tables.
* **Mourad E. H. Ismail (ed., with assistance from Walter Van Assche); Tom H.
  Koornwinder and Jasper V. Stokman (eds., volume II), *Encyclopedia of
  Special Functions: The Askey–Bateman Project*** (volumes I–II, 2020–2021:
  *Univariate Orthogonal Polynomials*; *Multivariable Special Functions*,
  [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9780511979156); [volume II](https://doi.org/10.1017/9780511777165)). A
  modern extension of the Bateman project, covering classical and basic
  orthogonal polynomials and multivariable functions. The two published
  volumes are cited together as a reference work.
* **Roelof Koekoek, Peter A. Lesky, and René F. Swarttouw,
  *Hypergeometric Orthogonal Polynomials and Their q-Analogues***
  (2010, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-05014-5)). Listed in full in Section 48.
* **Victor H. Moll, *Special Integrals of Gradshteyn and Ryzhik: The
  Proofs*** (2 volumes, 2014–2015, [CRC
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1201/b17674); [volume II](https://www.routledge.com/Special-Integrals-of-Gradshteyn-and-Ryzhik-the-Proofs---Volume-II/Moll/p/book/9780367377274)). Proofs of entries in the table,
  organised by method.
* **Takashi Takebe, *Elliptic Integrals and Elliptic Functions*** (2023,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-30265-7)). Elliptic integrals,
  elliptic functions, and their analytic relations. A recent graduate
  treatment of a classical subject that connects function theory with
  integrable systems.
* **Ranjan Roy, *Elliptic and Modular Functions from Gauss to Dedekind
  to Hecke*** (2017, [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781316671504)). Listed in full in Section 2.
* **George Dassios, *Ellipsoidal Harmonics: Theory and Applications*** (2012,
  [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9781139017749)).
  Lamé functions, ellipsoidal harmonics, and boundary-value problems in
  ellipsoidal geometry. A research reference for separation of variables
  beyond spherical coordinates.
* **Yoshishige Haraoka, *Linear Differential Equations in the Complex
  Domain: From Classical Theory to Forefront*** (2020,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-54663-2), Lecture Notes in
  Mathematics). Fuchsian equations, monodromy, rigidity, and middle
  convolution.
* **Rudolf Gorenflo, Anatoly A. Kilbas, Francesco Mainardi, and Sergei
  V. Rogosin, *Mittag-Leffler Functions, Related Topics and
  Applications*** (2nd ed., 2020, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-662-61550-8)). The monograph on
  Mittag-Leffler functions and their role in fractional equations.
* **Kai Diethelm, *The Analysis of Fractional Differential Equations:
  An Application-Oriented Exposition Using Differential Operators of
  Caputo Type*** (2010, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-14574-2)).
  Existence, uniqueness, and numerical approximation for fractional
  differential equations, with the fractional calculus and special functions
  they require.
* **Carlo Bardaro, Paul L. Butzer, Ilaria Mantellini, and Gerhard Schmeisser, *Mellin
  Analysis, Transform Theory, and Applications*** (2025,
  [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-96672-9)). The Mellin transform as a
  transform theory in its own right, with approximate identities and applications.

---

<a id="ckma-50"></a>

## 50. Asymptotic Analysis, Resurgence, and Perturbation

Asymptotic expansions of integrals and differential equations, stationary
phase, steepest descent, WKB, singular perturbations, Borel summability, and
resurgence describe limiting and exponentially small behaviour. Averaging
and spectral asymptotics supply related applications. Section 31 treats the
dynamical framework, Section 30 microlocal and semiclassical operator
theory, and Section 49 special functions. Riemann–Hilbert and exact
integrable methods are developed in Section 51, while geometric spectral
applications are in Section 53.

### Foundational works

#### Asymptotic expansions and integrals

* **F. W. J. Olver, *Asymptotics and Special Functions***. The
  foundational modern reference for asymptotic expansions, turning
  points, uniform asymptotics, and error bounds.
* **R. Wong, *Asymptotic Approximations of Integrals***. The standard
  detailed reference for Laplace, stationary phase, saddle points, and
  uniform methods.
* **N. G. de Bruijn, *Asymptotic Methods in Analysis***. A short
  classic on Laplace's method, saddle points, and implicit
  asymptotics.
* **Norman Bleistein and Richard A. Handelsman, *Asymptotic Expansions
  of Integrals***. A practical treatment of steepest descent and
  uniform approximation.
* **Arthur Erdélyi, *Asymptotic Expansions***. A short classical
  introduction to asymptotic expansions.
* **E. T. Copson, *Asymptotic Expansions***. A compact classical
  treatment, with the expansion of integrals done in detail.
* **R. B. Paris and D. Kaminski, *Asymptotics and Mellin–Barnes
  Integrals***. Mellin methods, with exponentially improved
  expansions.
* **R. B. Dingle, *Asymptotic Expansions: Their Derivation and
  Interpretation***. Divergent asymptotic expansions, late terms,
  terminants, and the interpretation of exponentially small contributions. A
  classical source for the analysis of the Stokes phenomenon.
* **Philippe Flajolet and Robert Sedgewick, *Analytic Combinatorics***
  (2009, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511801655)). Singularity analysis,
  saddle points, and Mellin transforms applied to generating functions.
  Complex analysis as a counting tool.
* **Vladimir I. Arnold, Sabir M. Gusein-Zade, and Alexander N. Varchenko, *Singularities of
  Differentiable Maps, Volume II: Monodromy and Asymptotics of
  Integrals***. Asymptotics of oscillatory integrals via singularity
  theory.
* **Jacob Korevaar, *Tauberian Theory: A Century of
  Developments***. Tauberian theorems from Hardy–Littlewood and Wiener to
  modern applications.

#### Differential equations, summability, and resurgence

* **M. V. Fedoryuk, *Asymptotic Analysis: Linear Ordinary Differential
  Equations*** and his work on the saddle-point method. The
  Russian-school treatment.
* **Wolfgang Wasow, *Asymptotic Expansions for Ordinary Differential
  Equations***. The classical reference for irregular singular points
  and turning-point theory.
  Also listed in Section 31.
* **Roscoe B. White, *Asymptotic Analysis of Differential Equations***
  (revised ed., 2010, [Imperial College Press](https://doi.org/10.1142/p735);
  first edition 2005). WKB methods, turning points, boundary layers, and
  complex asymptotic matching. Useful for connecting formal
  differential-equation approximations with integral representations.
* **Ovidiu Costin, *Asymptotics and Borel Summability***. The standard
  book-length treatment of resurgence and Borel summation for
  analysts.
* **Werner Balser, *Formal Power Series and Linear Systems of
  Meromorphic Ordinary Differential Equations***. Multisummability and
  the Stokes phenomenon, rigorously.
* **Takahiro Kawai and Yoshitsugu Takei, *Algebraic Analysis of Singular
  Perturbation Theory*** (2005, [American Mathematical
  Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/mmono/227), Translations of Mathematical
  Monographs). Exact WKB analysis, Borel resummation, and the Painlevé
  equations.
* **Robert Conte and Micheline Musette, *The Painlevé Handbook*** (2nd
  ed., 2020, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-53340-3); first
  edition 2008). A handbook of Painlevé equations, their solutions, and
  their asymptotics.
  Also listed in Section 51.
* **Peter D. Miller, *Applied Asymptotic Analysis***. A modern
  graduate course on Laplace's method, steepest descent and stationary
  phase, WKB and differential equations in the complex plane,
  boundary-value problems, and weakly nonlinear waves, with careful
  error control.
* **Wolfgang Wasow, *Linear Turning Point Theory***. Uniform asymptotics
  for linear differential equations with turning points.

#### Perturbation methods and averaging

* **Carl M. Bender and Steven A. Orszag, *Advanced Mathematical
  Methods for Scientists and Engineers***. Asymptotic methods for
  differential equations, including WKB, boundary layers, multiple scales,
  and matched expansions, with an emphasis on formal methods and
  applications.
* **Ali H. Nayfeh, *Perturbation Methods***. Multiple scales, matched
  asymptotics, and the standard applied perturbation methods.
* **J. Kevorkian and J. D. Cole, *Multiple Scale and Singular
  Perturbation Methods***. The systematic treatment of multiple-scale
  and singular perturbation methods.
* **Jan A. Sanders, Ferdinand Verhulst, and James Murdock, *Averaging
  Methods in Nonlinear Dynamical Systems*** (2nd ed., 2007, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-387-48918-6)).
  Listed in full in Section 31. The rigorous theory of averaging.
* **Robert E. O'Malley Jr., *Singular Perturbation Methods for Ordinary
  Differential Equations***. Boundary layers and matched expansions for
  ODE.
* **P. A. Lagerstrom, *Matched Asymptotic Expansions: Ideas and
  Techniques***. The conceptual foundations of matched asymptotic
  expansions.

#### Spectral asymptotics

* **Mouez Dimassi and Johannes Sjöstrand, *Spectral Asymptotics in the
  Semi-Classical Limit***. Asymptotics as spectral theory; see also
  Section 30.
  Also listed in Section 30.
* **Victor Ivrii, *Microlocal Analysis and Precise Spectral
  Asymptotics***. Microlocal methods for spectral asymptotics, including
  sharp remainder estimates beyond the leading Weyl term.
  Also listed in Section 53.

### More recent works

* **Nico M. Temme, *Asymptotic Methods for Integrals*** (2015, [World
  Scientific](https://doi.org/10.1142/9195)). Saddle points, uniform
  expansions, and the asymptotics of special functions, in the tradition of
  the DLMF.
* **R. B. Paris, *Hadamard Expansions and Hyperasymptotic Evaluation: An
  Extension of the Method of Steepest Descents*** (2011, [Cambridge
  University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511753626)). Convergent
  Hadamard expansions and exponentially improved asymptotics.
* **Claude Mitschi and David Sauzin, *Divergent Series, Summability and
  Resurgence I*** (2016, Springer, Lecture Notes in Mathematics). An
  introduction to divergent series, Borel summation, and resurgence, with
  applications to differential equations and dynamical systems.
* **Michèle Loday-Richaud, *Divergent Series, Summability and
  Resurgence II: Simple and Multiple Summability*** (2016, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-29075-1),
  Lecture Notes in Mathematics). Simple and multiple summability for formal
  solutions of differential equations, including Stokes phenomena. The
  second volume of the series begun by Mitschi and Sauzin.
* **Eric Delabaere, *Divergent Series, Summability and Resurgence III:
  Resurgent Methods and the First Painlevé Equation*** (2016, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-29000-3),
  Lecture Notes in Mathematics). Resurgence applied to Painlevé I.
* **Claude Sabbah, *Introduction to Stokes Structures*** (2013,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-31695-1), Lecture Notes in Mathematics). Stokes filtrations and the
  geometry of irregular singularities.
* **Naofumi Honda, Takahiro Kawai, and Yoshitsugu Takei, *Virtual
  Turning Points*** (2015, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-4-431-55702-9)). Exact WKB analysis and virtual
  turning points, in a short monograph.
* **Elena Shchepakina, Vladimir Sobolev, and Michael P. Mortell, *Singular
  Perturbations: Introduction to System Order Reduction Methods with
  Applications*** (2014,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-09570-7)). Slow manifolds,
  singular perturbations, and reduction of differential systems. A compact
  research account linking asymptotic expansions with dynamical-system
  geometry.
* **Robin Pemantle, Mark C. Wilson, and Stephen Melczer, *Analytic
  Combinatorics in Several Variables*** (2nd ed., 2024, [Cambridge
  University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781108874144)). Asymptotics of
  multivariate generating functions through residues, stratified Morse
  theory, and hyperbolic polynomials.
* **Victor Ivrii, *Microlocal Analysis, Sharp Spectral Asymptotics and
  Applications*** (5 volumes, 2019, Springer; [I](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-30557-4), [II](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-30541-3), [III](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-30537-6), [IV](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-30545-1), [V](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-30561-1)). Listed in full in Section 30.
  Spectral asymptotics at the precision the classical Weyl law does not
  reach.

---

<a id="ckma-51"></a>

## 51. Integrable Systems and Riemann–Hilbert Methods

Integrable equations are studied through inverse scattering, Hamiltonian and
Lax structures, spectral curves, transformations, and Riemann–Hilbert
problems. Continuous and discrete soliton equations, Painlevé equations, and
asymptotic and numerical Riemann–Hilbert methods are included. Section 34
develops general nonlinear-wave and dispersive PDE estimates, Section 41
complex differential equations, and Section 50 asymptotics and summability.
Orthogonal polynomials and random matrix applications connect with Sections
48 and 7B; broader inverse problems are linked through the cross-subject
index.

### Foundational works

* **Percy Deift, *Orthogonal Polynomials and Random Matrices: A
  Riemann–Hilbert Approach***. The standard introduction to the
  nonlinear steepest-descent method.
  Also listed in Sections 7 and 48.
* **Athanassios S. Fokas, Alexander R. Its, Andrei A. Kapaev, and
  Victor Yu. Novokshenov, *Painlevé Transcendents: The Riemann–Hilbert
  Approach***. Riemann–Hilbert methods for Painlevé equations, including
  monodromy, asymptotic analysis, and connection formulas.
* **Vladimir E. Zakharov, Sergei V. Manakov, Sergei P. Novikov, and
  Lev P. Pitaevskii, *Theory of Solitons: The Inverse Scattering
  Method***. Inverse scattering for integrable nonlinear equations, with
  soliton solutions and their spectral description.
* **Mark J. Ablowitz and Athanassios S. Fokas, *Complex Variables:
  Introduction and Applications***. A complex-analysis course leading to
  conformal mapping, asymptotic evaluation of integrals, and Riemann–Hilbert
  problems.
* **Ludwig D. Faddeev and Leon A. Takhtajan, *Hamiltonian Methods in
  the Theory of Solitons***. The Hamiltonian and spectral structure.
* **Olivier Babelon, Denis Bernard, and Michel Talon, *Introduction to
  Classical Integrable Systems*** (2003, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511535024)). Lax pairs, r-matrices,
  spectral curves, and their algebro-geometric and field-theoretic sides.
* **Vladimir A. Marchenko, *Sturm–Liouville Operators and
  Applications***. Inverse spectral theory, and the analytic core of
  the inverse scattering transform.
  Also listed in Section 22.
* **Fritz Gesztesy and Helge Holden, *Soliton Equations and Their
  Algebro-Geometric Solutions***, volume I, *(1+1)-Dimensional
  Continuous Models* (2003), and, with Johanna Michor and Gerald
  Teschl, volume II, *(1+1)-Dimensional Discrete Models* (2008,
  Cambridge University Press; [I](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511546723), [II](https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/soliton-equations-and-their-algebrogeometric-solutions/444985FF5EB231A3101AEBE8DEDAFB2B)). Algebro-geometric solutions of soliton
  equations, with the spectral theory done carefully.
* **Spyridon Kamvissis, Kenneth D. T.-R. McLaughlin, and Peter D. Miller,
  *Semiclassical Soliton Ensembles for the Focusing Nonlinear Schrödinger
  Equation*** (2003, [Princeton University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1515/9781400837182), Annals of Mathematics
  Studies). Nonlinear steepest descent in the semiclassical limit.
* **Jinho Baik, Thomas Kriecherbauer, Kenneth D. T.-R. McLaughlin, and Peter
  D. Miller, *Discrete Orthogonal Polynomials: Asymptotics and
  Applications*** (2007, [Princeton University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1515/9781400837137), Annals of Mathematics
  Studies). Riemann–Hilbert analysis for discrete weights, with applications
  to random tilings.
* **Valerii I. Gromak, Ilpo Laine, and Shun Shimomura, *Painlevé
  Differential Equations in the Complex Plane*** (2002, [De Gruyter](https://doi.org/10.1515/9783110198096)).
  Listed in full in Section 41.
* **Robert Conte and Micheline Musette, *The Painlevé Handbook*** (2nd
  ed., 2020, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-53340-3)). Listed in full in Section 50.
* **Alexander I. Bobenko and Yuri B. Suris, *Discrete Differential Geometry:
  Integrable Structure*** (2008, [American Mathematical
  Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/098), Graduate Studies in
  Mathematics). Discrete surfaces and nets whose multidimensional
  consistency is the discrete form of integrability.
* **Mark J. Ablowitz and Peter A. Clarkson, *Solitons, Nonlinear Evolution
  Equations and Inverse Scattering***. Inverse scattering, Painlevé
  equations, and integrable systems.
* **C. Rogers and W. K. Schief, *Bäcklund and Darboux
  Transformations***. Bäcklund and Darboux transformations in geometry and
  soliton theory.
* **Morikazu Toda, *Theory of Nonlinear Lattices***. The Toda lattice,
  its solutions, and its integrability.
* **Mark J. Ablowitz, Barbara Prinari, and A. David Trubatch, *Discrete and Continuous
  Nonlinear Schrödinger Systems***. Integrable discrete and continuous nonlinear Schrödinger
  equations and their inverse scattering. Distinct from Ablowitz and Clarkson, *Solitons,
  Nonlinear Evolution Equations and Inverse Scattering*.

### More recent works

* **Mark J. Ablowitz, *Nonlinear Dispersive Waves: Asymptotic Analysis and
  Solitons*** (2011, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511998324)). Asymptotic derivation of
  dispersive models, solitons, and inverse scattering, with physical examples.
  A graduate route into integrable wave equations from their analytic origins.
* **Thomas Trogdon and Sheehan Olver, *Riemann–Hilbert Problems, Their
  Numerical Solution, and the Computation of Nonlinear Special Functions***
  (2015, [SIAM](https://doi.org/10.1137/1.9781611974201)). Analytic
  foundations of Riemann–Hilbert problems developed toward reliable
  computation. Included for its operator theory, asymptotic analysis, and
  nonlinear special functions.
* **Athanassios S. Fokas and Beatrice Pelloni (eds.), *Unified Transform for
  Boundary Value Problems: Applications and Advances*** (2014,
  [SIAM](https://doi.org/10.1137/1.9781611973822)). Substantial treatments of
  the unified transform for linear and integrable nonlinear boundary problems.
  Complements Fokas's earlier single-author introduction with subsequent
  developments.
* **Jinho Baik, Percy Deift, and Toufic Suidan, *Combinatorics and
  Random Matrix Theory*** (2016, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/172)). Listed in full in Section 7. The Riemann–Hilbert method in a combinatorial setting.
* **Dan Romik, *The Surprising Mathematics of Longest Increasing
  Subsequences*** (2015, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9781139872003)). Ulam's problem from the
  hook formula to the Baik–Deift–Johansson theorem and the Tracy–Widom law.
* **Nalini Joshi, *Discrete Painlevé Equations*** (2019, [American
  Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/cbms/131), CBMS Regional Conference Series). Geometry,
  Lax pairs, and Riemann–Hilbert problems for discrete Painlevé
  equations.
  Also listed in Sections 41 and 48.
* **Jarmo Hietarinta, Nalini Joshi, and Frank W. Nijhoff, *Discrete Systems
  and Integrability*** (2016, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9781107337411)). Integrable difference
  equations, consistency around the cube, and discrete Painlevé equations.
* **Sergey M. Natanzon, *Complex Analysis, Riemann Surfaces and Integrable
  Systems*** (2019, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-34640-9)).
  Riemann surfaces, theta functions, and algebro-geometric methods for
  integrable equations. A graduate bridge between complex geometry and the
  analytic theory of integrability.
* **David Lannes, *The Water Waves Problem*** (2013, [American
  Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/surv/188)). Listed in full in Section 34. The analysis of the full water-wave equations and their
  asymptotic models, providing context for integrable wave approximations.
