# Part IX. Global and Geometric Analysis

---

<a id="ckma-52"></a>

## 52. Geometric Analysis on Manifolds

Riemannian and sub-Riemannian geometry shape problems in harmonic maps,
nonlinear elliptic equations, minimal surfaces, and geometric evolution.
Symplectic, gauge-theoretic, Lorentzian, and control methods are included
where their analytic foundations are central, together with comparison
theory and heat kernels. Section 37 supplies geometric measure theory,
Section 39 nonsmooth metric and diffusion counterparts, and Section 45
complex and Kähler analysis. Section 53 focuses on linear geometric
operators, index theory, and spectral geometry.

### Foundational works

#### Riemannian geometry, comparison, and heat kernels

* **John M. Lee, *Introduction to Riemannian Manifolds*** (2nd ed., 2018,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-91755-9), Graduate Texts in
  Mathematics; first edition 1997, as *Riemannian Manifolds: An
  Introduction to Curvature*). The standard first course in Riemannian
  geometry.
* **Jürgen Jost, *Riemannian Geometry and Geometric Analysis***. The
  standard graduate bridge from Riemannian geometry to harmonic maps,
  variational problems, and elliptic estimates.
* **Isaac Chavel, *Riemannian Geometry: A Modern
  Introduction***. Geometric foundations with substantial analytic
  content.
* **Peter Petersen, *Riemannian Geometry***. Primarily geometry, but
  the standard reference for the comparison theory that feeds into
  analysis.
* **Jeff Cheeger and David G. Ebin, *Comparison Theorems in Riemannian
  Geometry***. A focused treatment of comparison arguments in Riemannian
  geometry, including curvature, geodesics, and the variation of energy.
* **Isaac Chavel, *Eigenvalues in Riemannian Geometry***. The classic
  reference for spectral geometry and geometric eigenvalue
  inequalities.
  Also listed in Section 53.
* **Richard Schoen and Shing-Tung Yau, *Lectures on Differential
  Geometry***. Research-level geometry shaped throughout by PDE,
  curvature, and minimal-surface methods.
* **Alexander Grigor'yan, *Heat Kernel and Analysis on
  Manifolds***. Heat kernels, volume growth, parabolic estimates, and
  the analytic consequences of geometry.
  Also listed in Sections 14 and 39.
* **Jürgen Jost, *Partial Differential Equations*** and ***Nonpositive
  Curvature: Geometric and Analytic Aspects***. The analytic
  companions to Jost's geometry.
* **Luca Capogna, Donatella Danielli, Scott D. Pauls, and Jeremy T.
  Tyson, *An Introduction to the Heisenberg Group and the
  Sub-Riemannian Isoperimetric Problem*** (2007, [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-7643-8133-2)). The model
  case of the sub-Riemannian isoperimetric problem.
* **Andrei A. Agrachev and Yuri L. Sachkov, *Control Theory from the
  Geometric Viewpoint***. Controllability, the orbit theorem, and the
  Pontryagin maximum principle in the language of differential geometry.
  The geometric control theory that the later sub-Riemannian treatise
  assumes.
* **Velimir Jurdjevic, *Geometric Control Theory***. Controllability, the maximum principle,
  and optimal control on Lie groups and homogeneous spaces. Distinct from Agrachev and
  Sachkov, *Control Theory from the Geometric Viewpoint*.

#### Nonlinear elliptic problems and harmonic maps

* **Thierry Aubin, *Some Nonlinear Problems in Riemannian Geometry***
  and ***Nonlinear Analysis on Manifolds: Monge–Ampère
  Equations***. Sobolev inequalities, Yamabe-type problems, and
  complex Monge–Ampère on manifolds.
* **Emmanuel Hebey, *Nonlinear Analysis on Manifolds: Sobolev Spaces
  and Inequalities***. The functional-analytic complement to Aubin,
  careful about best constants.
  Also listed in Section 14.
* **Olivier Druet, Emmanuel Hebey, and Frédéric Robert, *Blow-up Theory
  for Elliptic PDEs in Riemannian Geometry*** (2004, [Princeton
  University Press](https://doi.org/10.1515/9781400826162), Mathematical Notes). Blow-up analysis
  for critical elliptic equations on manifolds.
* **Sun-Yung Alice Chang, *Non-linear Elliptic Equations in Conformal
  Geometry*** (2004, [European Mathematical
  Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/006), Zurich Lectures in Advanced
  Mathematics). Conformally covariant operators, Q-curvature, and extremal
  metrics.
* **Fanghua Lin and Changyou Wang, *The Analysis of Harmonic Maps and
  Their Heat Flows***. Harmonic maps, partial regularity,
  singularities, and evolution.
* **Frédéric Hélein, *Harmonic Maps, Conservation Laws and Moving
  Frames***. Compensated compactness and regularity for harmonic maps.

#### Geometric flows

* **Peter Topping, *Lectures on the Ricci Flow***. A short introduction to Ricci
  flow, its basic estimates, and its geometric applications.
* **Bennett Chow, Peng Lu, and Lei Ni, *Hamilton's Ricci Flow***. The
  detailed reference for Ricci flow as a partial differential
  equation.
* **Bennett Chow, Sun-Chin Chu, David Glickenstein, Christine Guenther,
  James Isenberg, Tom Ivey, Dan Knopf, Peng Lu, Feng Luo, and Lei Ni,
  *The Ricci Flow: Techniques and Applications*** (four parts,
  2007–2015, American Mathematical Society; [I](https://doi.org/10.1090/surv/135), [II](https://doi.org/10.1090/surv/144), [III](https://doi.org/10.1090/surv/163), [IV](https://doi.org/10.1090/surv/206)). The technical companion to
  *Hamilton's Ricci Flow*.
* **John Morgan and Gang Tian, *Ricci Flow and the Poincaré
  Conjecture*** (2007, [American Mathematical Society](https://www.claymath.org/resource/ricci-flow-and-the-poincare-conjecture/)).
  The detailed exposition of Perelman's proof. Its sequel, *The
  Geometrization Conjecture* (2014), is listed among the more recent works.
* **Klaus Ecker, *Regularity Theory for Mean Curvature Flow***. The
  research monograph on regularity for mean curvature flow.

#### Minimal surfaces

* **Robert Osserman, *A Survey of Minimal Surfaces***. A classical
  compact survey of enduring value.
* **H. Blaine Lawson, Jr., *Lectures on Minimal Submanifolds***. A
  classical geometric treatment.
* **Ulrich Dierkes, Stefan Hildebrandt, and Friedrich Sauvigny,
  *Minimal Surfaces*** (revised 2010 edition of the 1992 book by Dierkes,
  Hildebrandt, Küster, and Wohlrab) and the companion volumes *Regularity
  of Minimal Surfaces* and *Global Analysis of Minimal Surfaces* (with
  Tromba). Broad PDE and regularity
  coverage of the Plateau problem.
* **Michael Struwe, *Plateau's Problem and the Calculus of
  Variations***. Short and focused on the variational analysis.

#### Symplectic, gauge-theoretic, and Lorentzian analysis

* **Dusa McDuff and Dietmar A. Salamon, *Introduction to Symplectic Topology***
  (3rd ed., 2017, [Oxford University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1093/oso/9780198794899.001.0001); first edition
  1995). Symplectic manifolds, capacities, and Floer-theoretic ideas, with
  the analytic background. The companion to their *J-holomorphic Curves*.
* **Dusa McDuff and Dietmar A. Salamon, *J-holomorphic Curves and
  Symplectic Topology*** (2nd ed., 2012, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/coll/052);
  first edition 2004). Pseudoholomorphic curves. Included because the
  analysis — elliptic regularity, bubbling, and transversality — is the
  content analysts use.
* **Liviu I. Nicolaescu, *An Invitation to Morse Theory*** (2nd ed.,
  2011, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4614-1105-5); first edition
  2007). Morse theory with the analytic transversality made explicit.
* **Peter Kronheimer and Tomasz Mrowka, *Monopoles and
  Three-Manifolds*** (2007, [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511543111)). Seiberg–Witten
  monopoles and the analytic estimates behind the topological
  applications.
* **Katrin Wehrheim, *Uhlenbeck Compactness*** (2004, [European Mathematical
  Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/004)). Complete proofs of Uhlenbeck's
  gauge-fixing and compactness theorems.
* **Christian Bär, Nicolas Ginoux, and Frank Pfäffle, *Wave Equations on
  Lorentzian Manifolds and Quantization*** (2007, [European Mathematical
  Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/037)). Fundamental solutions and Green
  operators for normally hyperbolic operators on globally hyperbolic
  spacetimes.

### More recent works

#### Riemannian, sub-Riemannian, and conformal analysis

* **Peter Li, *Geometric Analysis*** (2012, Cambridge University
  Press). Gradient estimates, harmonic functions, eigenvalue bounds,
  and analysis on complete manifolds.
* **Andrei A. Agrachev, Davide Barilari, and Ugo Boscain, *A Comprehensive
  Introduction to Sub-Riemannian Geometry*** (2020, [Cambridge
  University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781108677325)). A systematic
  treatment of sub-Riemannian geometry, from geodesics and optimal control
  to the heat equation. Agrachev and Sachkov, *Control Theory from the
  Geometric Viewpoint*, is listed under Foundational works.
* **Emmanuel Hebey, *Compactness and Stability for Nonlinear Elliptic
  Equations*** (2014, [European Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/134), Zurich Lectures in
  Advanced Mathematics). Compactness and stability of the Yamabe-type
  equations, in a research course.
* **Gábor Székelyhidi, *An Introduction to Extremal Kähler Metrics***
  (2014, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/152)). Listed in full in Section 45.
* **Dan A. Lee, *Geometric Relativity*** (2019, [American Mathematical
  Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/201), Graduate Studies in
  Mathematics). Positive mass theorems, Penrose inequalities, and the
  constraint equations, treated as geometric analysis.

#### Geometric flows

* **John Morgan and Gang Tian, *The Geometrization Conjecture*** (2014,
  [American Mathematical Society](https://www.claymath.org/resource/the-geometrization-conjecture/),
  Clay Mathematics Monographs). The sequel to *Ricci Flow and the Poincaré
  Conjecture*, completing the exposition of Perelman's proof of
  Thurston's geometrization conjecture.
* **Ben Andrews and Christopher Hopper, *The Ricci Flow in Riemannian
  Geometry*** (2011, Springer, Lecture Notes in Mathematics). A concise
  geometric introduction to Ricci flow.
* **Simon Brendle, *Ricci Flow and the Sphere Theorem*** (2010,
  [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/111), Graduate Studies in Mathematics). The
  differentiable sphere theorem and the Ricci-flow arguments around it.
* **Bennett Chow, *Ricci Solitons in Low Dimensions*** (2023, [American
  Mathematical Society](https://www.ams.org/books/gsm/235/)). Ricci solitons
  in dimensions two and three and their role in Ricci-flow singularities. A
  focused graduate introduction before the broader research literature on
  geometric evolution.
* **Carlo Mantegazza, *Lecture Notes on Mean Curvature Flow*** (2011,
  Birkhäuser, Progress in Mathematics). An accessible analytic
  treatment of mean curvature flow.
* **Ben Andrews, Bennett Chow, Christine Guenther, and Mat Langford,
  *Extrinsic Geometric Flows*** (2020, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/206),
  Graduate Studies in Mathematics). Curve shortening and mean curvature
  flow, as a graduate treatise.

#### Minimal surfaces

* **Tobias H. Colding and William P. Minicozzi II, *A Course in
  Minimal Surfaces*** (2011, American Mathematical Society, Graduate
  Studies in Mathematics). The modern analytic theory of minimal
  surfaces.
* **William H. Meeks III and Joaquín Pérez, *A Survey on Classical
  Minimal Surface Theory*** (2012, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/ulect/060),
  University Lecture Series). The modern survey of classical minimal
  surface theory after Osserman and Lawson.
  Also listed in Section 37.
* **Antonio Alarcón, Franc Forstnerič, and Francisco J. López, *Minimal
  Surfaces from a Complex Analytic Viewpoint*** (2021, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-69056-4)).
  Listed in full in Section 45.

#### Symplectic and gauge-theoretic analysis

* **Michèle Audin and Mihai Damian, *Morse Theory and Floer Homology***
  (2014, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4471-5496-9),
  Universitext; French original 2010). Morse homology and a complete construction of Hamiltonian
  Floer homology, with the analysis done in detail.
* **Leonid Polterovich and Daniel Rosen, *Function Theory on Symplectic
  Manifolds*** (2014, [American Mathematical
  Society](https://www.ams.org/books/crmm/034/)). Poisson brackets,
  quasi-states, and rigidity of functions on symplectic manifolds. Included
  for its functional-analytic and variational viewpoint on symplectic
  geometry.
* **Kai Cieliebak and Yakov Eliashberg, *From Stein to Weinstein and Back:
  Symplectic Geometry of Affine Complex Manifolds*** (2012, [American
  Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/coll/059), Colloquium
  Publications). Stein manifolds, plurisubharmonic functions, and Weinstein
  structures. Also relevant to Section 45.

---

<a id="ckma-53"></a>

## 53. Elliptic Operators on Manifolds, Index Theory, and Spectral Geometry

Differential operators on manifolds are studied through elliptic estimates,
Hodge theory, Dirac operators, index theorems, heat-kernel asymptotics,
eigenfunctions, and spectral geometry. Boundary conditions, higher index
methods, and geometric inverse problems extend this global operator
viewpoint. Section 22 develops abstract spectral theory, Section 30
microlocal calculi, and Section 52 nonlinear geometric analysis.
Complex-geometric
applications also connect with Section 45, and inverse problems with the
cross-subject index.

### Foundational works

#### Elliptic operators, Hodge theory, and partial differential relations

* **Michael E. Taylor, *Partial Differential Equations I–II***. Elliptic theory
  on manifolds, Hodge theory, and related geometric applications within a
  broader PDE course.
* **Frank W. Warner, *Foundations of Differentiable Manifolds and Lie
  Groups***. The standard background, including a careful treatment of
  the Hodge theorem.
* **Liviu I. Nicolaescu, *Lectures on the Geometry of Manifolds***. A
  large modern course covering elliptic operators, Hodge theory, and
  index theory in one place.
* **M. A. Shubin, *Pseudodifferential Operators and Spectral
  Theory***. Global symbol calculus and spectral asymptotics.
  Also listed in Section 30.
* **Mikhail Gromov, *Partial Differential Relations***. A research treatise on
  the h-principle, partial differential relations, and flexibility methods
  in differential geometry.

#### Index theory and Dirac operators

* **John Roe, *Elliptic Operators, Topology and Asymptotic
  Methods***. Elliptic operators and index theory developed through heat
  kernels and asymptotic methods, with the necessary geometric background.
* **Peter B. Gilkey, *Invariance Theory, the Heat Equation, and the
  Atiyah–Singer Index Theorem***. Heat-kernel asymptotics, invariant theory,
  and their use in proving index theorems.
* **Nicole Berline, Ezra Getzler, and Michèle Vergne, *Heat Kernels
  and Dirac Operators***. The standard reference for the local index
  theorem and its supersymmetric proof.
* **H. Blaine Lawson, Jr. and Marie-Louise Michelsohn, *Spin
  Geometry***. The comprehensive reference for Dirac operators and
  their analysis.
* **Thomas Friedrich, *Dirac Operators in Riemannian Geometry*** (2000,
  [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/025), Graduate
  Studies in Mathematics). Spin geometry, Dirac operators, and eigenvalue
  estimates.
* **Bernhelm Booss-Bavnbek and Krzysztof P. Wojciechowski, *Elliptic
  Boundary Problems for Dirac Operators***. Boundary value problems
  and spectral flow.
* **Richard B. Melrose, *The Atiyah–Patodi–Singer Index Theorem*** and
  ***Geometric Scattering Theory***. Microlocal analysis on manifolds
  with boundary and with ends.
  Also listed in Section 30.
* **Nigel Higson and John Roe, *Analytic K-Homology*** (2000, Oxford
  University Press). Listed in full in Section 23.
* **Wolfgang Lück, *L²-Invariants: Theory and Applications to Geometry and
  K-Theory*** (2002, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-662-04687-6),
  Ergebnisse). L²-Betti numbers, L²-torsion, and the Atiyah conjecture: von
  Neumann algebras applied to topology.
* **Xiaonan Ma and George Marinescu, *Holomorphic Morse Inequalities and
  Bergman Kernels*** (2007, [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-7643-8115-8)). Bergman kernel asymptotics and
  holomorphic Morse inequalities.
* **Jean-Michel Bismut and Gilles Lebeau, *The Hypoelliptic Laplacian
  and Ray–Singer Metrics*** (2008, [Princeton University Press](https://doi.org/10.1515/9781400829064), Annals
  of Mathematics Studies). The hypoelliptic Laplacian and its relation
  to the Ray–Singer torsion.
* **Nicolas Ginoux, *The Dirac Spectrum***. Eigenvalue estimates for the Dirac operator on
  Riemannian spin manifolds. Distinct from Bär, Ginoux, and Pfäffle, *Wave Equations on
  Lorentzian Manifolds and Quantization*.

#### Spectral geometry

* **Isaac Chavel, *Eigenvalues in Riemannian Geometry***. The classic
  reference for eigenvalues and the geometry of the Laplacian.
  Also listed in Section 52.
* **Pierre H. Bérard, *Spectral Geometry: Direct and Inverse
  Problems***. Direct and inverse spectral geometry in the classical
  style.
* **Peter B. Gilkey, *Asymptotic Formulae in Spectral Geometry*** (2004,
  [CRC Press](https://doi.org/10.1201/9780203490464)). Heat-trace and
  eta-invariant asymptotics, with boundary conditions.
* **Victor Ivrii, *Microlocal Analysis and Precise Spectral
  Asymptotics***. Microlocal methods for sharp Weyl laws and spectral
  remainder estimates. Also listed in Section 50.
* **Christopher D. Sogge, *Hangzhou Lectures on Eigenfunctions of the
  Laplacian*** and ***Fourier Integrals in Classical
  Analysis***. Harmonic-analytic eigenfunction estimates.
* **Henryk Iwaniec, *Spectral Methods of Automorphic Forms*** (2nd ed.,
  2002, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/053),
  Graduate Studies in Mathematics; first edition 1995). Spectral theory of
  the Laplacian on hyperbolic surfaces, Eisenstein series, and the Selberg
  trace formula.
* **David Borthwick, *Spectral Theory of Infinite-Area Hyperbolic
  Surfaces*** (2nd ed., 2016, [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-33877-4); first edition 2007). Listed in full in Section 22.
* **Antoine Henrot, *Extremum Problems for Eigenvalues of Elliptic
  Operators***. The extremal side of spectral geometry.
  Also listed in Section 14.
* **Antoine Henrot and Michel Pierre, *Shape Variation and
  Optimization*** (2018, [European Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/178); French original
  2005). Listed in full in Section 36. Extremal eigenvalues as a
  shape-optimisation problem.
* **Steven Rosenberg, *The Laplacian on a Riemannian Manifold***. An
  introduction to Hodge theory, heat kernels, and the heat-equation proof
  of the Chern–Gauss–Bonnet theorem.

### More recent works

* **Michael Levitin, Dan Mangoubi, and Iosif Polterovich, *Topics in Spectral
  Geometry*** (2023, [American Mathematical
  Society](https://www.ams.org/publications/authors/books/postpub/gsm-237)).
  Eigenvalues, eigenfunctions, variational principles, and the geometry of the
  Laplacian. A modern graduate course complementing the classical manifold and
  index-theory references.
* **Nicolas Bergeron, *The Spectrum of Hyperbolic Surfaces*** (2016,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-27666-3); French original
  2011). Selberg, geodesic flows, and the spectrum of hyperbolic
  surfaces, as a graduate text. Also listed in Section 44.
* **Steve Zelditch, *Eigenfunctions of the Laplacian on a Riemannian
  Manifold*** (2017, American Mathematical Society, CBMS Regional
  Conference Series). The modern survey of eigenfunction concentration,
  quantum ergodicity, and nodal sets.
* **Victor Ivrii, *Microlocal Analysis, Sharp Spectral Asymptotics and
  Applications*** (5 volumes, 2019, Springer; [I](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-30557-4), [II](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-30541-3), [III](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-30537-6), [IV](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-30545-1), [V](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-30561-1)). Listed in full in Section 30.
* **Gabriel P. Paternain, Mikko Salo, and Gunther Uhlmann, *Geometric
  Inverse Problems*** (2023, [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781009039901)).
  Listed in full in Section 22. Geodesic X-ray transforms, tensor tomography, and
  boundary rigidity, with emphasis on two-dimensional manifolds.
* **David D. Bleecker and Bernhelm Booss-Bavnbek, *[Index Theory with
  Applications to Mathematics and Physics](https://thiele.ruc.dk/~booss/A-S-Index-Book/Index_12_01.htm)*** (2013, International Press).
  The index theorem through several proofs and many examples.
* **Rufus Willett and Guoliang Yu, *Higher Index Theory*** (2020,
  [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781108867351)). Listed in full in Section 23.
* **Michael Ruzhansky, Makhmud Sadybekov, and Durvudkhan Suragan, *Spectral Geometry of
  Partial Differential Operators*** (2020, [Chapman and
  Hall/CRC](https://doi.org/10.1201/9780429432965)). Spectral geometry of differential
  operators on domains, including boundary conditions and explicit spectra. Distinct from
  Ruzhansky and Suragan, *Hardy Inequalities on Homogeneous Groups*.

# Part X. The Working Apparatus

---

<a id="ckma-54"></a>

## 54. Problem Books, Counterexamples, and Reference Tables

Problem collections, counterexamples, tables, and encyclopedic resources
supply practice, tests of hypotheses, explicit formulas, and reference
material across analysis. This is a classification by use rather than a
separate mathematical subfield: specialised works are also listed in the
subject sections where their content is developed. Section 1 provides broad
systematic courses, Section 2 historical perspective, and Section 49 the
main special-function and integral-transform references. Research-problem
collections are distinguished from instructional exercises.

### Foundational works

* **G. Pólya and G. Szegő, *Problems and Theorems in Analysis
  I–II***. A classical problem collection developing techniques in real and
  complex analysis through worked problems and their solutions.
  Also listed in Section 11.
* **Boris M. Makarov, M. G. Goluzina, A. A. Lodkin, and Anatolii N. Podkorytov,
  *Selected Problems in Real Analysis***. A collection of challenging
  problems in real-variable analysis, including differentiation,
  integration, and measure theory.
* **W. J. Kaczor and M. T. Nowak, *Problems in Mathematical Analysis
  I–III***. A large graded collection with full solutions.
* **Charalambos D. Aliprantis and Owen Burkinshaw, *Problems in Real
  Analysis***. The companion to their *Principles of Real Analysis*,
  and a good source of routine-but-instructive exercises.
* **Paul R. Halmos, *A Hilbert Space Problem Book***. Hilbert-space and operator
  theory developed through problems, with hints and solutions.
  Also listed in Section 21.
* **Konrad Knopp, *Problem Book in the Theory of Functions
  I–II***. The classical complex-analysis problem collection.
* **Albert N. Shiryaev, *Problems in Probability***, translated by
  Andrew Lyasoff (2012, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4614-3688-1)). A large problem collection across
  graduate probability, with solutions.
* **Bernard R. Gelbaum and John M. H. Olmsted, *Counterexamples in
  Analysis***. The standard compact source of pathology.
* **Lynn Arthur Steen and J. Arthur Seebach, Jr., *Counterexamples in
  Topology***. Examples distinguishing topological properties, useful for
  checking hypotheses and implications in analysis.
* **Jordan M. Stoyanov, *Counterexamples in Probability*** (3rd ed., 2013,
  [Dover](https://store.doverpublications.com/products/9780486315935)). The standard counterexample collection for probability.
* **Ralph P. Boas, Jr., *A Primer of Real Functions***. Short,
  example-driven, and full of useful pathology.
* **J. Michael Steele, *The Cauchy–Schwarz Master Class***. Inequality
  techniques developed through problems and applications, beginning with
  Cauchy–Schwarz and extending to related classical inequalities.
* **Walter Rudin, *Real and Complex Analysis*** and ***Functional
  Analysis***. Listed in full in Sections 1, 4, and 40. The exercises provide additional practice in
  measure theory, complex analysis, and functional analysis.
* **The Bateman Manuscript Project volumes**. The special-function and
  integral-transform reference volumes listed in Section 49, with extensive
  classical identities and representations.
* **I. S. Gradshteyn and I. M. Ryzhik, *Table of Integrals, Series,
  and Products***. A reference collection of integrals, series, and
  products, complementing the special-function references in Section 49.
  Also listed in Section 49.
* **A. P. Prudnikov, Yu. A. Brychkov, and O. I. Marichev, *Integrals
  and Series***. Extensive tables of integrals, series, and integral
  transforms. Listed in full in Section 49.
* **Andrei N. Borodin and Paavo Salminen, *Handbook of Brownian Motion —
  Facts and Formulae*** (2nd ed., 2002,
  [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-0348-8163-0)). Tables of
  distributions and functionals of Brownian motion and related diffusions,
  with supporting formulas and references.
* **Encyclopedia of Mathematics (Springer/EMS, online)** and **Wolfram
  Functions Site**. Useful for orientation and for formula checking
  respectively; both to be verified against a primary source.

### More recent works

* **Barry Simon, *A Comprehensive Course in Analysis*** (2015, American
  Mathematical Society). The problem sets and notes make the series a
  standing reference beyond its exposition.
  Also listed in Section 1.
* **René L. Schilling and Franziska Kühn, *Counterexamples in Measure
  and Integration*** (2021, [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781009003797)). Listed in full in Section 4.
* **Stephan Ramon Garcia, Javad Mashreghi, and William T. Ross,
  *Operator Theory by Example*** (2023, [Oxford University Press](https://doi.org/10.1093/oso/9780192863867.001.0001)).
  Listed in full in Section 21. Operator theory taught by worked examples.
* **Paolo Baldi, *Stochastic Calculus*** (2017, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-62226-2)).
  Listed in full in Section 8. A stochastic-calculus course with an extensive
  collection of exercises and solutions.
* **W. K. Hayman and Eleanor F. Lingham, *Research Problems in Function
  Theory*** (2019, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-25165-9)).
  Listed in full in Section 41. A collection of research problems in classical
  function theory, with commentary on their background and status at
  publication.
* **[NIST Digital Library of Mathematical Functions](https://dlmf.nist.gov/)** (2010, NIST).
  An online reference for special-function definitions, identities,
  differential equations, and asymptotics, complementing the older printed
  tables.
* **Roelof Koekoek, Peter A. Lesky, and René F. Swarttouw,
  *Hypergeometric Orthogonal Polynomials and Their q-Analogues***
  (2010, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-05014-5)). Listed in full in Section 48. The catalogue of
  formulae for the Askey scheme, corresponding to the DLMF for special
  functions.

---

<a id="ckma-55"></a>

## 55. Foundations, Constructive Analysis, and Machine-Formalized Mathematics

Alternative foundations and computational approaches to rigorous analysis
are separated into constructive, computable, and proof-theoretic methods
(55A), set-theoretic and nonstandard foundations (55B), formalized
mathematics and proof-assistant libraries (55C), and validated numerics and
computer-assisted proof (55D). Formal proof and verified numerical enclosure
have distinct methods even when combined. Section 3 supplies general
topological and descriptive foundations; the subject sections supply the
classical mathematics formalized or analysed by these approaches.

<a id="ckma-55a"></a>

### 55A. Constructive, Computable, and Proof-Theoretic Analysis

Constructive mathematics, computability, reverse mathematics, algorithmic
randomness, and proof mining examine existence, effectiveness, and the
logical strength of analytic theorems.

#### Foundational works

* **Errett Bishop and Douglas S. Bridges, *Constructive Analysis***. A systematic
  development of Bishop-style constructive analysis, including measure
  theory, functional analysis, and complex analysis.

* **Douglas S. Bridges and Luminiţa Simona Vîţă, *Techniques of
  Constructive Analysis***. A modern, readable continuation.

* **Anne Sjerp Troelstra and Dirk van Dalen, *Constructivism in
  Mathematics I–II***. The systematic logical background.

* **Stephen G. Simpson, *Subsystems of Second Order Arithmetic***. Reverse
  mathematics in subsystems of second-order arithmetic, identifying the
  axioms needed for many classical theorems of analysis.

* **Klaus Weihrauch, *Computable Analysis***. The type-two theory of
  effectivity; the standard framework for computability over the
  reals.

* **Marian B. Pour-El and J. Ian Richards, *Computability in Analysis
  and Physics***. The classical account of computable and
  noncomputable phenomena in analysis.

* **André Nies, *Computability and Randomness*** (2009, [Oxford University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1093/acprof:oso/9780199230761.001.0001)).
  Algorithmic randomness and lowness notions; the companion to
  Downey–Hirschfeldt.

* **Ulrich Kohlenbach, *Applied Proof Theory: Proof Interpretations and
  their Use in Mathematics*** (2008, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-540-77533-1)). Proof mining and the
  extraction of quantitative bounds from analytic proofs.

#### More recent works

* **Douglas S. Bridges, Hajime Ishihara, Michael Rathjen, and Helmut
  Schwichtenberg (eds.), *Handbook of Constructive Mathematics*** (2023,
  [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781009039888)). A
  substantial handbook of constructive analysis, topology, foundations, and
  computation. Supplies a modern research panorama beyond the Bishop–Bridges
  and Troelstra–van Dalen books.

* **Yuen-Kwok Chan, *Foundations of Constructive Probability Theory*** (2021,
  [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781108884013)).
  Probability developed constructively, with explicit attention to the
  information needed to establish convergence and existence. A specialised
  continuation of Bishop-style analysis.

* **Denis R. Hirschfeldt, *Slicing the Truth*** (2014, World Scientific).
  An introduction to computability-theoretic methods in reverse mathematics,
  emphasising the relative strength of mathematical principles.

* **Damir D. Dzhafarov and Carl Mummert, *Reverse Mathematics: Problems,
  Reductions, and Proofs*** (2022,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-11367-3)). Modern reverse
  mathematics, with computable reducibilities between problems.

* **Vasco Brattka and Peter Hertling (eds.), *Handbook of Computability
  and Complexity in Analysis*** (2021, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-59234-9)). The research handbook
  of computable analysis: computable reals, computable functionals, and
  the complexity of analytic operations.

* **Rodney G. Downey and Denis R. Hirschfeldt, *Algorithmic Randomness and
  Complexity*** (2010, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-387-68441-3)).
  A major research reference on effective randomness and its connections with
  computability and measure. Supplies the foundational background for
  effective probabilistic analysis.

* **Johanna N. Y. Franklin and Christopher P. Porter (eds.), *Algorithmic
  Randomness: Progress and Prospects*** (2020, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781108781718)). Research surveys on
  effective randomness, computability, and connections with analysis. A later
  continuation of the Downey–Hirschfeldt monograph.

* **Helmut Schwichtenberg and Stanley S. Wainer, *Proofs and
  Computations*** (2012, [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9781139031905)). Primitive
  recursion, computability, and the proof theory that constructive
  analysis uses.

<a id="ckma-55b"></a>

### 55B. Set-Theoretic and Nonstandard Foundations

Axiomatic structures, hyperreal and hyperfinite constructions, and Loeb
measures supply foundational frameworks and alternative methods for
classical analysis.

#### Foundational works

* **Abraham Robinson, *Non-standard Analysis***. The founding account
  of nonstandard analysis.

* **Albert E. Hurd and Peter A. Loeb, *An Introduction to Nonstandard
  Real Analysis***. Nonstandard real analysis and the Loeb measure
  construction, with applications in probability and functional
  analysis.

* **Peter A. Loeb and Manfred P. H. Wolff (eds.), *Nonstandard Analysis for
  the Working Mathematician*** (2nd ed., 2015,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-94-017-7327-0)). Nonstandard
  methods in measure theory, functional analysis, probability, and
  economics.

* **Sergio Albeverio, Jens Erik Fenstad, Raphael Høegh-Krohn, and Tom
  Lindstrøm, *Nonstandard Methods in Stochastic Analysis and
  Mathematical Physics***. Nonstandard methods applied to stochastic
  analysis and mathematical physics, connecting hyperfinite constructions
  with classical analytic models.

* **Nicolas Bourbaki, *Theory of Sets***. Formal mathematics, axiomatic set
  theory, ordered sets, cardinals, and Bourbaki's theory of structures.
  Provides foundational background for the organisation of abstract
  mathematics.

* **Robert Goldblatt, *Lectures on the Hyperreals: An Introduction to
  Nonstandard Analysis***. The ultrapower construction of the hyperreals
  and nonstandard analysis.

* **Vladimir Kanovei and Michael Reeken, *Nonstandard Analysis, Axiomatically***. An
  axiomatic development of nonstandard analysis for use in analysis. Distinct from Kanovei's
  *Borel Equivalence Relations*.

<a id="ckma-55c"></a>

### 55C. Formalized Mathematics and Proof-Assistant Libraries

Machine-checked definitions, theorem statements, and proofs provide reusable
formal libraries of analysis in interactive proof assistants.

#### More recent works

* **The Mathlib library and its documentation**. A Lean library of
  machine-checked mathematics, including topology, measure and integration,
  functional analysis, complex analysis, and Fourier analysis. Its generated
  documentation records formal definitions, theorem statements, and their
  dependencies.

* **The Coq/Rocq analysis libraries (Coquelicot, MathComp-Analysis)**.
  Libraries of formal real analysis for Coq/Rocq, including limits,
  differentiation, and integration. Their documentation provides access to
  machine-checked definitions and theorems.

<a id="ckma-55d"></a>

### 55D. Validated Numerics and Computer-Assisted Proof

Interval bounds, verified approximation, and numerical enclosure methods
turn computation into rigorous existence, error, and dynamical conclusions.

#### More recent works

* **Warwick Tucker, *Validated Numerics: A Short Introduction to Rigorous
  Computations*** (2011, [Princeton University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1515/9781400838974)). Interval arithmetic and
  computer-assisted proofs in analysis and dynamics.

* **Mitsuhiro T. Nakao, Michael Plum, and Yoshitaka Watanabe, *Numerical
  Verification Methods and Computer-Assisted Proofs for Partial Differential
  Equations*** (2019,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-981-13-7669-6)). Verified numerical
  methods for PDE, especially nonlinear elliptic boundary-value problems,
  with rigorous error bounds and computer-assisted existence proofs.
  Includes extensions to evolution problems.


---

# Suggested Reading Paths

No analyst reads a bibliography linearly. The more useful way to
interpret "core knowledge" is by layers.

## A. First graduate year: a common foundation

1. **Real analysis and measure**: Folland, Rudin, Royden–Fitzpatrick,
   or Stein–Shakarchi *Real Analysis*.
2. **Functional analysis**: Conway, Rudin, Brezis, or Stein–Shakarchi
   *Functional Analysis*.
3. **Complex analysis**: Ahlfors, Conway, Stein–Shakarchi, Gamelin, or
   Remmert.
4. **PDE**: Evans, Taylor I, Folland, or Renardy–Rogers.
5. **Probability, where it is part of the programme**: Billingsley,
   Durrett, Williams, or Kallenberg used selectively.

By the end of this stage one should be fluent in distributions, weak
convergence, Sobolev spaces, the Fourier transform, and basic spectral
theory, whether or not any course was labelled with those names.

## B. Second-course infrastructure

The books that most change how a graduate student actually proves
things:

- Stein, *Singular Integrals*, and Grafakos I;
- Adams–Fournier or Leoni on Sobolev spaces;
- Bergh–Löfström on interpolation;
- Hörmander I on distributions and Fourier analysis;
- Kato on operators, and Reed–Simon I–II;
- Engel–Nagel or Pazy on semigroups;
- Gilbarg–Trudinger on elliptic PDE;
- Evans–Gariepy on fine properties;
- Rockafellar on convex analysis, and Bauschke–Combettes on monotone
  operators;
- Kallenberg or Durrett for probability, with Ledoux–Talagrand or
  Boucheron–Lugosi–Massart for concentration;
- Villani, *Topics in Optimal Transportation*, which has become a
  common second language.

## C. Research entry by direction

**Harmonic analysis.** Stein's two monographs and Grafakos I–II, then
Muscalu–Schlag, Cruz-Uribe–Martell–Pérez for the
sparse and weighted theory, Demeter for decoupling, and Wolff or
Mattila for the geometric side.

**Linear and nonlinear PDE.** Hörmander I–IV; Gilbarg–Trudinger,
Ladyzhenskaya–Ural'tseva, and Krylov for elliptic and parabolic
regularity; Caffarelli–Cabré and Crandall–Ishii–Lions for the fully
nonlinear theory; Dafermos and Bressan for conservation laws; Tao,
Cazenave, and Bahouri–Chemin–Danchin for dispersive equations.

**Functional and operator analysis.** Kato, Reed–Simon,
Dunford–Schwartz; Sz.-Nagy–Foiaş and Nikolski for model theory;
Takesaki and Kadison–Ringrose for operator algebras;
Lindenstrauss–Tzafriri, Pisier, and Albiac–Kalton for Banach spaces;
Hytönen–van Neerven–Veraar–Weis for the vector-valued theory.

**Geometric measure theory and variational analysis.** Maggi and Simon
first, then Federer as a reference; Ambrosio–Fusco–Pallara for BV;
Dacorogna and Dal Maso for variational convergence;
Ambrosio–Gigli–Savaré and Villani for transport and gradient flows;
David–Semmes and Tolsa for quantitative rectifiability.

**Complex analysis, one variable.** Burckel or Remmert for the
classical theory in full; Garnett and Duren for Hardy spaces;
Pommerenke and Garnett–Marshall for boundary behaviour and harmonic
measure; Levin and Koosis for entire functions and the logarithmic
integral; Nikolski for the operator-theoretic direction.

**Complex analysis, several variables.** Hörmander and Range first;
then Chen–Shaw and Straube for the d-bar-Neumann problem; Demailly and
Forstnerič for the geometric and Oka-theoretic directions; Klimek and
Guedj–Zeriahi for pluripotential theory; Baouendi–Ebenfelt–Rothschild
for CR mappings.

**Probability and stochastic analysis.** Kallenberg, Revuz–Yor,
Rogers–Williams, Ethier–Kurtz, Stroock–Varadhan; then Nualart for
Malliavin calculus, Friz–Hairer for rough paths, and Da Prato–Zabczyk
for SPDE.

**Special functions and asymptotics.** Andrews–Askey–Roy and the DLMF;
Olver and Wong for asymptotics; Ismail and Simon for orthogonal
polynomials; Costin or Balser for summability; Deift for the
Riemann–Hilbert method.

**Spectral theory and mathematical physics.** Reed–Simon and Kato;
Teschl or Schmüdgen for a modern foundation; Zworski and
Dimassi–Sjöstrand for the semiclassical theory; Dyatlov–Zworski for
resonances.

## D. A note on how to use a list like this

Most of these books will never be read completely by anyone, and that
is not a defect. The practical skill is knowing which book a given
question belongs to. It is worth spending an hour with the table of
contents of a major reference before needing it, so that later the
memory to be retrieved is "this is a Maz'ya question" or "this is a
Burckel question" rather than a search.

---

# What This Bibliography Deliberately Does Not Try to Do

- **Neighbouring fields appear only where they support analytic
  practice.** Algebraic geometry, differential topology,
  representation theory, and logic are represented by the specific
  books analysts actually open.
- **Numerical analysis and scientific computing are largely omitted**,
  despite their deep interaction with approximation theory, PDE, and
  spectral theory. A few exceptions are admitted where the book is the
  best available account of a piece of analysis (Trefethen, de Boor,
  Peyré–Cuturi).
- **Mathematical statistics is omitted** except where it coincides
  with probability, empirical processes, or asymptotics.
- **Analytic number theory is omitted as a subject**, although many of
  its tools are harmonic or complex analytic; Montgomery's *Ten
  Lectures* is included precisely because it sits on the
  boundary. Readers wanting the subject itself should begin with
  Iwaniec–Kowalski.
- **Mathematical physics is included only where the analysis is the
  point** — spectral theory, scattering, operator-algebraic
  statistical mechanics, kinetic theory — and not otherwise.
- **Specialized literatures in stochastic PDE, random matrices,
  inverse problems, integrable systems, and free probability are
  represented by their nearest stable monographs** rather than by
  article lists, even where the research front is entirely in papers.
- **Research areas whose canonical literature is still primarily
  periodical** are represented by lecture notes and surveys where
  these have stabilized, and are otherwise omitted rather than guessed
  at.
- **No claim of completeness or of ranking is made.** Several
  excellent books are absent because they never became common
  reference points, and a few unfashionable books are present because
  they still are.

The operative test throughout: a book belongs here if knowing that it
exists, and roughly what role it plays, is part of the working culture
of graduate or research-level analysis.
